of the post-quantum Internet in 2025&quot;],&quot;slug&quot;:[0,&quot;pq-2025&quot;],&quot;excerpt&quot;:[0,&quot;Today over half of human-initiated traffic with Cloudflare is protected against harvest-now/decrypt-later with post-quantum encryption. What once was a cool science project, is the new security baseline for the Internet. Weâre not done yet: in this blog post weâll take measure where we are, what we expect for the coming years, and what you can do today.&quot;],&quot;featured&quot;:[0,false],&quot;html&quot;:[0,&quot;&lt;p&gt;&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;This week, the last week of October 2025, we reached a major milestone for Internet security: the majority of human-initiated traffic with Cloudflare is &lt;a href=\&quot;https://radar.cloudflare.com/adoption-and-usage#post-quantum-encryption\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;using&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; post-quantum encryption mitigating the &lt;a href=\&quot;https://blog.cloudflare.com/the-quantum-menace/\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;threat&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; of &lt;a href=\&quot;https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harvest_now,_decrypt_later\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;harvest-now/decrypt-later&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt;\n          &lt;figure class=\&quot;kg-card kg-image-card\&quot;&gt;\n          &lt;Image src=\&quot;https://cf-assets.www.cloudflare.com/zkvhlag99gkb/1EUFTKSnJptvd5WDGvB9Rf/4865f75c71e43f2c261d393322d24f34/image5.png\&quot; alt=\&quot;Graph showing percentage of human traffic protected by post-quantum encryption.\&quot; class=\&quot;kg-image\&quot; width=\&quot;1250\&quot; height=\&quot;550\&quot; loading=\&quot;lazy\&quot;/&gt;\n          &lt;/figure&gt;&lt;p&gt;We want to use this joyous moment to give an update on the current state of the migration of the Internet to post-quantum cryptography and the long road ahead. Our last &lt;a href=\&quot;https://blog.cloudflare.com/pq-2024/\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;overview&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; was 21 months ago, and quite a lot has happened since. A lot of it has been passed as we &lt;a href=\&quot;https://blog.cloudflare.com/pq-2024/\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;predicted&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;: finalization of the NIST standards; broad adoption of post-quantum encryption; more detailed roadmaps from regulators; progress on building quantum computers; some cryptography was broken (not to worry: nothing close to whatâs deployed); and new exciting cryptography was proposed.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;But there were also a few surprises: there was a giant leap in progress towards Q-day by improving quantum algorithms, and we had a proper scare because of a new quantum algorithm. Weâll cover all this and more: what we expect for the coming years; and what you can do today.&lt;/p&gt;\n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h2 id=\&quot;the-quantum-threat\&quot;&gt;The quantum threat&lt;/h2&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#the-quantum-threat\&quot; aria-hidden=\&quot;true\&quot; class=\&quot;relative sm:absolute sm:-left-5\&quot;&gt;\n        &lt;svg width=\&quot;16\&quot; height=\&quot;16\&quot; viewBox=\&quot;0 0 24 24\&quot;&gt;&lt;path fill=\&quot;currentcolor\&quot; d=\&quot;m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z\&quot;&gt;&lt;/path&gt;&lt;/svg&gt;\n      &lt;/a&gt;\n    &lt;/div&gt;\n    &lt;p&gt;First things first: why are we changing our cryptography? Itâs because of &lt;b&gt;quantum computers&lt;/b&gt;. &lt;a href=\&quot;https://www.cloudflare.com/learning/ssl/quantum/what-is-quantum-computing/\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;These marvelous devices&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;, instead of restricting themselves to zeroes and ones, compute using more of what nature actually affords us: quantum superposition, interference, and entanglement. This allows quantum computers to excel at certain very specific computations, notably simulating nature itself, which will be very helpful in developing new materials.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;Quantum computers are not going to replace regular computers, though: theyâre actually much worse than regular computers at most tasks that matter for our daily lives. Think of them as graphic cards or neural engines â specialized devices for specific computations, not general-purpose ones.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;Unfortunately, quantum computers also &lt;a href=\&quot;https://blog.cloudflare.com/the-quantum-menace\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;excel&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; at breaking key cryptography that still is in common use today, such as RSA and elliptic curves (ECC). Thus, we are moving to &lt;b&gt;post-quantum cryptography&lt;/b&gt;: cryptography designed to be resistant against quantum attack. Weâll discuss the exact impact on the different types of cryptography later on.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;For now, quantum computers are rather anemic: theyâre simply not good enough today to crack any real-world cryptographic keys. That doesnât mean we shouldnât worry yet: encrypted traffic can be &lt;a href=\&quot;https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harvest_now,_decrypt_later\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;harvested today&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;, and decrypted after &lt;b&gt;Q-day&lt;/b&gt;: the day that quantum computers are capable of breaking todayâs still widely used cryptography such as RSA-2048. We call that a âharvest-now-decrypt-laterâ attack.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;Using factoring as a benchmark, quantum computers donât impress at all: the largest number factored by a quantum computer without cheating is 15, a record thatâs easily beaten in a &lt;a href=\&quot;https://eprint.iacr.org/2025/1237.pdf\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;variety of funny ways&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;. Itâs tempting to disregard quantum computers until they start beating classical computers on factoring, but that would be a big mistake. Even conservative estimates place Q-day &lt;a href=\&quot;https://youtu.be/nJxENYdsB6c?si=doosb_aZRpQgo6X8&amp;t=1302\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;less than three years&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; after the day that quantum computers beat classical computers on factoring. So how do we track progress?&lt;/p&gt;\n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h3 id=\&quot;quantum-numerology\&quot;&gt;Quantum numerology&lt;/h3&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#quantum-numerology\&quot; aria-hidden=\&quot;true\&quot; class=\&quot;relative sm:absolute sm:-left-5\&quot;&gt;\n        &lt;svg width=\&quot;16\&quot; height=\&quot;16\&quot; viewBox=\&quot;0 0 24 24\&quot;&gt;&lt;path fill=\&quot;currentcolor\&quot; d=\&quot;m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z\&quot;&gt;&lt;/path&gt;&lt;/svg&gt;\n      &lt;/a&gt;\n    &lt;/div&gt;\n    &lt;p&gt;There are two categories to consider in the march towards Q-day: progress on quantum hardware, and algorithmic improvements to the software that runs on that hardware. We have seen significant progress on both fronts.&lt;/p&gt;\n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h4 id=\&quot;progress-on-quantum-hardware\&quot;&gt;Progress on quantum hardware&lt;/h4&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#progress-on-quantum-hardware\&quot; aria-hidden=\&quot;true\&quot; class=\&quot;relative sm:absolute sm:-left-5\&quot;&gt;\n        &lt;svg width=\&quot;16\&quot; height=\&quot;16\&quot; viewBox=\&quot;0 0 24 24\&quot;&gt;&lt;path fill=\&quot;currentcolor\&quot; d=\&quot;m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z\&quot;&gt;&lt;/path&gt;&lt;/svg&gt;\n      &lt;/a&gt;\n    &lt;/div&gt;\n    &lt;p&gt;Like clockwork, every year there are news stories of new quantum computers with record-breaking number of qubits. This focus on counting qubits is also quite misleading. To start, quantum computers are analogue machines, and there is always some noise interfering with the computation.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;There are big differences between the different types of technology used to build quantum computers: &lt;a href=\&quot;https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transmon\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;silicon-based&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; quantum computers seem to scale well, are quick to execute instructions, but have very noisy qubits. This does not mean theyâre useless: with &lt;a href=\&quot;https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quantum_error_correction\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;quantum error correcting codes&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; one can effectively turn millions of noisy silicon qubits into a few thousand high-fidelity ones, which could be enough to &lt;a href=\&quot;https://quantum-journal.org/papers/q-2021-04-15-433/\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;break RSA&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;. &lt;a href=\&quot;https://www.quantinuum.com/products-solutions/quantinuum-systems\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;Trapped-ion quantum computers&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;, on the other hand, have much less noise, but have been harder to scale. Only a few hundred-thousand trapped-ion qubits could potentially draw the curtain on RSA-2048.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;div style=\&quot;position: relative; padding-top: 52.42937853107345%;\&quot;&gt;\n  &lt;iframe\n    src=\&quot;https://customer-eq7kiuol0tk9chox.cloudflarestream.com/63767af9e4ed768f95acc0c267179a00/iframe?preload=true&amp;loop=true&amp;autoplay=true&amp;poster=https%3A%2F%2Fcustomer-eq7kiuol0tk9chox.cloudflarestream.com%2F63767af9e4ed768f95acc0c267179a00%2Fthumbnails%2Fthumbnail.jpg%3Ftime%3D%26height%3D600\&quot;\n    loading=\&quot;lazy\&quot;\n    style=\&quot;border: none; position: absolute; top: 0; left: 0; height: 100%; width: 100%;\&quot;\n    allow=\&quot;accelerometer; gyroscope; autoplay; encrypted-media; picture-in-picture;\&quot;\n    allowfullscreen=\&quot;true\&quot;\n  &gt;&lt;/iframe&gt;\n&lt;/div&gt;\n&lt;p&gt;&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;&lt;sup&gt;Timelapse of &lt;/sup&gt;&lt;a href=\&quot;https://sam-jaques.appspot.com/quantum_landscape\&quot;&gt;&lt;sup&gt;&lt;u&gt;state-of-art&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/sup&gt;&lt;/a&gt;&lt;sup&gt; in quantum computing from 2021 through 2025 by qubit count on the x-axis and noise on the y-axis. The dots in the gray area are the various quantum computers out there. Once the shaded gray area hits the left-most red line, weâre in trouble as that means a quantum computer can break large RSA keys. Compiled by &lt;/sup&gt;&lt;a href=\&quot;https://sam-jaques.appspot.com/\&quot;&gt;&lt;sup&gt;&lt;u&gt;Samuel Jaques&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/sup&gt;&lt;/a&gt;&lt;sup&gt; of the University of Waterloo.&lt;/sup&gt;&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;Weâre only scratching the surface with the number of qubits and noise. There are low-level details that can make a big difference, such as the interconnectedness of qubits. More importantly, the graph doesnât capture how scalable the engineering behind the records is.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;To wit, on these graphs the progress on quantum computers seems to have stalled the last two years, whereas for experts, Googleâs &lt;a href=\&quot;https://blog.google/technology/research/google-willow-quantum-chip/\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;December 2024 Willow announcement&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; that is unremarkable on the graph, is in reality a &lt;a href=\&quot;https://scottaaronson.blog/?p=8525\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;real milestone&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; achieving the first logical qubit in the surface code in a scalable manner. &lt;a href=\&quot;https://sam-jaques.appspot.com/quantum_landscape_2024\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;Quoting&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; Sam Jaques:&lt;/p&gt;&lt;blockquote&gt;&lt;p&gt;When I first read these results [Willowâs achievements], I felt chills of âOh wow, quantum computing is actually realâ.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/blockquote&gt;&lt;p&gt;Itâs a real milestone, but not an unexpected leap. Quoting Sam again:&lt;/p&gt;&lt;blockquote&gt;&lt;p&gt;Despite my enthusiasm, this is more or less where we should expect to be, and maybe a bit late. All of the big breakthroughs they demonstrated are steps we needed to take to even hope to reach the 20 million qubit machine that could break RSA. There are no unexpected breakthroughs. Think of it like the increases in transistor density of classical chips each year: an impressive feat, but ultimately business-as-usual.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/blockquote&gt;&lt;p&gt;Business-as-usual is also the strategy: the superconducting qubit approach pursued by Google for Willow has always had the clearest path forward attacking the difficulties head-on requiring fewest leaps in engineering.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;Microsoft pursues the opposite strategy with their bet on &lt;a href=\&quot;https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Topological_quantum_computer\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;topological qubits&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;. These are qubits that in theory would mostly not be unaffected by noise. However, they have not been fully realized in hardware. If these can be built in a scalable way, theyâd be far superior to superconducting qubits. But we donât even know if these can be built to begin with. Early 2025 Microsoft announced the &lt;a href=\&quot;https://scottaaronson.blog/?p=8669\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;Majorana 1&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; chip, which demonstrates how these could be built. The chip is far from a full demonstrator though: it doesnât support any computation and hence doesnât even show up in Samâs comparison graph earlier.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;In between topological and superconducting qubits, there are many other approaches that labs across the world pursue that do show up in the graph, such as QuEra with &lt;a href=\&quot;https://www.quera.com/neutral-atom-platform\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;neutral atoms&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; and Quantinuum with &lt;a href=\&quot;https://www.quantinuum.com/products-solutions/quantinuum-systems/system-model-h2\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;trapped ions&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;Progress on the hardware side of getting to Q-day has received by far the most amount of press interest. The biggest breakthrough in the last two years isnât on the hardware side though.&lt;/p&gt;\n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h3 id=\&quot;progress-on-quantum-software\&quot;&gt;Progress on quantum software&lt;/h3&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#progress-on-quantum-software\&quot; d=\&quot;m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z\&quot;&gt;&lt;/path&gt;&lt;/svg&gt;\n      &lt;/a&gt;\n    &lt;/div&gt;\n    \n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h4 id=\&quot;the-biggest-breakthrough-so-far-craig-gidneys-optimisations\&quot;&gt;The biggest breakthrough so far: Craig Gidneyâs optimisations&lt;/h4&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#the-biggest-breakthrough-so-far-craig-gidneys-optimisations\&quot; aria-hidden=\&quot;true\&quot; class=\&quot;relative sm:absolute sm:-left-5\&quot;&gt;\n        &lt;svg width=\&quot;16\&quot; height=\&quot;16\&quot; viewBox=\&quot;0 0 24 24\&quot;&gt;&lt;path fill=\&quot;currentcolor\&quot; d=\&quot;m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z\&quot;&gt;&lt;/path&gt;&lt;/svg&gt;\n      &lt;/a&gt;\n    &lt;/div&gt;\n    &lt;p&gt;We thought weâd need about &lt;a href=\&quot;https://quantum-journal.org/papers/q-2021-04-15-433/\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;20 million qubits&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; with the superconducting approach to break RSA-2048. It turns out we can do it with much less. In a stunningly comprehensive June 2025 paper, &lt;a href=\&quot;https://algassert.com/about.html\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;Craig Gidney&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; shows that with clever quantum software optimisations we need fewer than &lt;a href=\&quot;https://arxiv.org/pdf/2505.15917\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;one million qubits&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;. This is the reason the red lines in Samâs graph above, marking the size of a quantum computer to break RSA, dramatically shift to the left in 2025.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;To put this achievement into perspective, letâs just make a wild guess and say Google can maintain a sort of Mooreâs law and doubles the number of physical qubits every one-and-a-half years. Thatâs a much faster pace than Google demonstrated so far, but itâs also not unthinkable they could achieve this once the groundwork has been laid. Then itâd take until 2052 to reach 20 million qubits, but only until 2045 to reach one million: Craig single-handedly brought Q-day &lt;b&gt;seven years&lt;/b&gt; closer!&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;How much further can software optimisations go? Pushing it lower than 100,000 superconducting qubits seems impossible to Sam, and &lt;a href=\&quot;https://sam-jaques.appspot.com/quantum_landscape_2025\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;heâd expect&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; more than 242,000 superconducting qubits are required to break RSA-2048. With the wild guess on quantum computer progress before, thatâd correspond to a Q-day of 2039 and 2041+ respectively.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;Although Craigâs estimate makes detailed and reasonable assumptions on the architecture of a large-scale superconducting qubits quantum computer, itâs still a guess, and these estimates could be off quite a bit.&lt;/p&gt;\n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h4 id=\&quot;a-proper-scare-chens-algorithm\&quot;&gt;A proper scare: Chenâs algorithm&lt;/h4&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#a-proper-scare-chens-algorithm\&quot; aria-hidden=\&quot;true\&quot; class=\&quot;relative sm:absolute sm:-left-5\&quot;&gt;\n        &lt;svg width=\&quot;16\&quot; height=\&quot;16\&quot; viewBox=\&quot;0 0 24 24\&quot;&gt;&lt;path fill=\&quot;currentcolor\&quot; d=\&quot;m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z\&quot;&gt;&lt;/path&gt;&lt;/svg&gt;\n      &lt;/a&gt;\n    &lt;/div&gt;\n    &lt;p&gt;On the algorithmic side, we might not only see improvements to existing quantum algorithms, but also the discovery of completely new quantum algorithms. April 2024, Yilei Chen published &lt;a href=\&quot;https://eprint.iacr.org/2024/555\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;a preprint&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; claiming to have found such a new quantum algorithm to solve certain lattice problems, which are close, but not the same as those we rely on for the post-quantum cryptography we deploy. This caused a proper stir: even if it couldnât attack our post-quantum algorithms today, could Chenâs algorithm be improved? To get a sense for potential improvements, you need to understand what the algorithm is really doing on a higher level. With Chenâs algorithm thatâs hard, as itâs very complex, much more so than Shorâs quantum algorithm that breaks RSA. So it took some time for experts to &lt;a href=\&quot;https://nigelsmart.github.io/LWE.html\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;start&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; &lt;a href=\&quot;https://sam-jaques.appspot.com/static/files/555-notes.pdf\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;seeing&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; limitations to Chenâs approach, and in fact, after ten days they discovered a fundamental bug in the algorithm: the approach doesnât work. Crisis averted.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;What to take from this? Optimistically, this is business as usual for cryptography, and lattices are in a better shape now as one avenue of attack turned out to be a dead end. Realistically, it &lt;i&gt;is&lt;/i&gt; a reminder that we have a lot of eggs in the lattices basket. As weâll see later, presently there isnât a real alternative that works everywhere.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;Proponents of quantum key distribution (QKD) might chime in that QKD solves exactly that by being secure thanks to the laws of nature. Well, there are some asterixes to put on that claim, but more fundamentally no one has figured out how to scale QKD beyond point-to-point connections, &lt;a href=\&quot;https://blog.cloudflare.com/you-dont-need-quantum-hardware/\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;as we argue in this blog post&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;Itâs good to speculate about what cryptography might be broken by a completely new attack, but letâs not forget the matter at hand: a lot of cryptography is going to be broken by quantum computers for sure. Q-day is coming; the question is when.&lt;/p&gt;\n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h2 id=\&quot;is-q-day-always-fifteen-years-away\&quot;&gt;Is Q-day always fifteen years away?&lt;/h2&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#is-q-day-always-fifteen-years-away\&quot; aria-hidden=\&quot;true\&quot; class=\&quot;relative sm:absolute sm:-left-5\&quot;&gt;\n        &lt;svg width=\&quot;16\&quot; height=\&quot;16\&quot; viewBox=\&quot;0 0 24 24\&quot;&gt;&lt;path fill=\&quot;currentcolor\&quot; d=\&quot;m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z\&quot;&gt;&lt;/path&gt;&lt;/svg&gt;\n      &lt;/a&gt;\n    &lt;/div&gt;\n    &lt;p&gt;If you&amp;#39;ve been working on or around cryptography and security long enough, then you have probably heard that &amp;quot;Q-day is X years away&amp;quot; every year for the last several years. This can make it feel like Q-day is always &amp;quot;some time in the future&amp;quot; â until we put such a claim in the proper context.&lt;/p&gt;\n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h3 id=\&quot;what-do-experts-think\&quot;&gt;What do experts think?&lt;/h3&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#what-do-experts-think\&quot; aria-hidden=\&quot;true\&quot; class=\&quot;relative sm:absolute sm:-left-5\&quot;&gt;\n        &lt;svg width=\&quot;16\&quot; height=\&quot;16\&quot; viewBox=\&quot;0 0 24 24\&quot;&gt;&lt;path fill=\&quot;currentcolor\&quot; d=\&quot;m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z\&quot;&gt;&lt;/path&gt;&lt;/svg&gt;\n      &lt;/a&gt;\n    &lt;/div&gt;\n    &lt;p&gt;Since 2019, the &lt;a href=\&quot;https://globalriskinstitute.org/\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;Global Risk Institute&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; has performed a yearly survey amongst experts, asking how probable it is that RSA-2048 will be broken within 5, 10, 15, 20 or 30 years. These are the results &lt;a href=\&quot;https://globalriskinstitute.org/publication/2024-quantum-threat-timeline-report/\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;for 2024&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;, whose interviews happened before Willowâs release and Gidneyâs breakthrough.&lt;/p&gt;\n          &lt;figure class=\&quot;kg-card kg-image-card\&quot;&gt;\n          &lt;Image src=\&quot;https://cf-assets.www.cloudflare.com/zkvhlag99gkb/3dx58nMhiJJd3DsQkaHwYF/84e9d8781912925d3b745f50291b00df/image6.png\&quot; alt=\&quot;Global Risk Institute expert survey results from 2024 on the likelihood of a quantum computer breaking RSA-2048 within different timelines.\&quot; class=\&quot;kg-image\&quot; width=\&quot;978\&quot; height=\&quot;676\&quot; loading=\&quot;lazy\&quot;/&gt;\n          &lt;/figure&gt;&lt;p&gt;&lt;sup&gt;Global Risk Institute expert survey results from 2024 on the likelihood of a quantum computer breaking RSA-2048 within different timelines.&lt;/sup&gt;&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;As the middle column in this chart shows, well over half of the interviewed experts thought there was at least a ~50% chance that a quantum computer will break RSA-2048 within 15 years. Letâs look up the historical answers from &lt;a href=\&quot;https://globalriskinstitute.org/publication/quantum-threat-timeline/\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;2019&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;, &lt;a href=\&quot;https://globalriskinstitute.org/publication/quantum-threat-timeline-report-2020/\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;2020&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;, &lt;a href=\&quot;https://globalriskinstitute.org/publication/2021-quantum-threat-timeline-report-global-risk-institute-global-risk-institute/\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;2021&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;, &lt;a href=\&quot;https://globalriskinstitute.org/publication/2022-quantum-threat-timeline-report/\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;2022&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;, and &lt;a href=\&quot;https://globalriskinstitute.org/publication/2023-quantum-threat-timeline-report/\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;2023&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;. Here we plot the likelihood for Q-day within 15 years (of the time of the interview):&lt;/p&gt;\n          &lt;figure class=\&quot;kg-card kg-image-card\&quot;&gt;\n          &lt;Image src=\&quot;https://cf-assets.www.cloudflare.com/zkvhlag99gkb/4rMVWq9lDr49n9BmDkH2Ye/73d14f83f553becedf29dd11ce25deb1/image10.png\&quot; alt=\&quot;Historical answers in the quantum threat timeline reports for the chance of Q-day within 15 years.\&quot; class=\&quot;kg-image\&quot; width=\&quot;640\&quot; height=\&quot;480\&quot; loading=\&quot;lazy\&quot;/&gt;\n          &lt;/figure&gt;&lt;p&gt;&lt;sup&gt;Historical answers in the quantum threat timeline reports for the chance of Q-day within 15 years.&lt;/sup&gt;&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;This shows that answers are slowly trending to more certainty, but at the rate we would expect? With six years of answers, we can plot how consistent the predictions are over a year: does the 15-year estimate for 2019 match the 10-year estimate for 2024?&lt;/p&gt;\n          &lt;figure class=\&quot;kg-card kg-image-card\&quot;&gt;\n          &lt;Image src=\&quot;https://cf-assets.www.cloudflare.com/zkvhlag99gkb/1cc2fWho4kYRjhJebG6Vll/12fdb65939b8e0143606d04747cfcca9/Screenshot_2025-10-28_at_12.28.49.png\&quot; alt=\&quot;Historical answers in the quantum threat timeline report over the years on the date of Q-day. The x-axis is the alleged year for Q-day and the y-axis shows the fraction of interviewed experts that think itâs at least ~50% (left) or 70% (right) likely to happen then.\&quot; class=\&quot;kg-image\&quot; width=\&quot;1280\&quot; height=\&quot;500\&quot; loading=\&quot;lazy\&quot;/&gt;\n          &lt;/figure&gt;&lt;p&gt;&lt;sup&gt;Historical answers in the quantum threat timeline report over the years on the date of Q-day. The x-axis is the alleged year for Q-day and the y-axis shows the fraction of interviewed experts that think itâs at least ~50% (left) or 70% (right) likely to happen then.&lt;/sup&gt;&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;If we ask experts when Q-day could be with about even odds (graph on the left), then they mostly keep saying the same thing over the years: yes, could be 15 years away. However, if we press for more certainty, and ask for Q-day with &amp;gt;70% probability (graph on the right), then the experts are mostly consistent over the years. For instance: one-fifth thought 2034 both in the 2019 and 2024 interviews.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;So, if you want a consistent answer from an expert, donât ask them when Q-day could be, but when itâs probably there. Now, itâs good fun to guess about Q-day, but the honest answer is that no one really knows for sure: there are just too many unknowns. And in the end, the date of Q-day is far less important than the deadlines set by regulators.&lt;/p&gt;\n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h3 id=\&quot;what-action-do-regulators-take\&quot;&gt;What action do regulators take?&lt;/h3&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#what-action-do-regulators-take\&quot; aria-hidden=\&quot;true\&quot; class=\&quot;relative sm:absolute sm:-left-5\&quot;&gt;\n        &lt;svg width=\&quot;16\&quot; height=\&quot;16\&quot; viewBox=\&quot;0 0 24 24\&quot;&gt;&lt;path fill=\&quot;currentcolor\&quot; d=\&quot;m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z\&quot;&gt;&lt;/path&gt;&lt;/svg&gt;\n      &lt;/a&gt;\n    &lt;/div&gt;\n    &lt;p&gt;We can also look at the timelines of various regulators. In 2022, the National Security Agency (NSA) released their &lt;a href=\&quot;https://media.defense.gov/2025/May/30/2003728741/-1/-1/0/CSA_CNSA_2.0_ALGORITHMS.PDF\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;CNSA 2.0 guidelines&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;, which has deadlines between 2030 and 2033 for migrating to post-quantum cryptography. Also in 2022, the US federal government &lt;a href=\&quot;http://web.archive.org/web/20240422052137/https://www.whitehouse.gov/briefing-room/statements-releases/2022/05/04/national-security-memorandum-on-promoting-united-states-leadership-in-quantum-computing-while-mitigating-risks-to-vulnerable-cryptographic-systems/\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;set 2035&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; as the target to have the United States fully migrated, from which the new administration hasnât deviated. In 2024 Australia set 2030 as their &lt;a href=\&quot;https://www.theregister.com/2024/12/17/australia_dropping_crypto_keys/\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;aggressive deadline&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; to migrate. Early 2025, the UK NCSC matched the common &lt;a href=\&quot;https://www.ncsc.gov.uk/guidance/pqc-migration-timelines\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;2035&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; as the deadline for the United Kingdom. Mid-2025, the European Union published &lt;a href=\&quot;https://digital-strategy.ec.europa.eu/en/library/coordinated-implementation-roadmap-transition-post-quantum-cryptography\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;their roadmap&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; with 2030 and 2035 as deadlines depending on the application.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;Far from all national regulators have provided post-quantum migration timelines, but those that do generally stick to the 2030â2035 timeframe.&lt;/p&gt;\n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h3 id=\&quot;when-is-q-day\&quot;&gt;When is Q-day?&lt;/h3&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#when-is-q-day\&quot; aria-hidden=\&quot;true\&quot; class=\&quot;relative sm:absolute sm:-left-5\&quot;&gt;\n        &lt;svg width=\&quot;16\&quot; height=\&quot;16\&quot; viewBox=\&quot;0 0 24 24\&quot;&gt;&lt;path fill=\&quot;currentcolor\&quot; d=\&quot;m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z\&quot;&gt;&lt;/path&gt;&lt;/svg&gt;\n      &lt;/a&gt;\n    &lt;/div&gt;\n    &lt;p&gt;So when will quantum computers start causing trouble? Whether itâs 2034 or 2050, for sure it will be &lt;b&gt;too soon&lt;/b&gt;. The immense success of cryptography over fifty years means itâs all around us now, from dishwasher, to pacemaker, to satellite. Most upgrades will be easy, and fit naturally in the productâs lifecycle, but there will be a long tail of difficult and costly upgrades.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;Now, letâs take a look at the migration to post-quantum cryptography.&lt;/p&gt;\n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h2 id=\&quot;mitigating-the-quantum-threat-two-migrations\&quot;&gt;Mitigating the quantum threat: two migrations&lt;/h2&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#mitigating-the-quantum-threat-two-migrations\&quot; aria-hidden=\&quot;true\&quot; class=\&quot;relative sm:absolute sm:-left-5\&quot;&gt;\n        &lt;svg width=\&quot;16\&quot; height=\&quot;16\&quot; viewBox=\&quot;0 0 24 24\&quot;&gt;&lt;path fill=\&quot;currentcolor\&quot; d=\&quot;m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z\&quot;&gt;&lt;/path&gt;&lt;/svg&gt;\n      &lt;/a&gt;\n    &lt;/div&gt;\n    &lt;p&gt;To help prioritize, it is important to understand that there is a big difference in the difficulty, impact, and urgency of the post-quantum migration for the different kinds of cryptography required to create secure connections. In fact, for most organizations there will be two post-quantum migrations: &lt;b&gt;key agreement&lt;/b&gt; and &lt;b&gt;signatures / certificates&lt;/b&gt;. Letâs explain this for the case of creating a secure connection when visiting a website in a browser.&lt;/p&gt;\n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h3 id=\&quot;already-post-quantum-secure-symmetric-cryptography\&quot;&gt;Already post-quantum secure: symmetric cryptography&lt;/h3&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#already-post-quantum-secure-symmetric-cryptography\&quot; aria-hidden=\&quot;true\&quot; class=\&quot;relative sm:absolute sm:-left-5\&quot;&gt;\n        &lt;svg width=\&quot;16\&quot; height=\&quot;16\&quot; viewBox=\&quot;0 0 24 24\&quot;&gt;&lt;path fill=\&quot;currentcolor\&quot; d=\&quot;m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z\&quot;&gt;&lt;/path&gt;&lt;/svg&gt;\n      &lt;/a&gt;\n    &lt;/div&gt;\n    &lt;p&gt;The cryptographic workhorse of a connection is a &lt;b&gt;symmetric cipher &lt;/b&gt;such as AES-GCM. Itâs what you would think of when thinking of cryptography: both parties, in this case the browser and server, have a shared key, and they encrypt / decrypt their messages with the same key. Unless you have that key, you canât read anything, or modify anything.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;The good news is that symmetric ciphers, such as &lt;a href=\&quot;https://blog.cloudflare.com/go-crypto-bridging-the-performance-gap/\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;AES-GCM&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;, are already post-quantum secure. There is a common misconception that &lt;a href=\&quot;https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grover%27s_algorithm\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;Groverâs quantum algorithm&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; requires us to double the length of symmetric keys. On closer inspection of the algorithm, itâs clear that it is &lt;a href=\&quot;https://blog.cloudflare.com/nist-post-quantum-surprise#grover-s-algorithm\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;not&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; &lt;a href=\&quot;https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=eB4po9Br1YY\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;practical&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;. The way &lt;a href=\&quot;https://www.nist.gov/\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;NIST&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;, the US National Institute for Standards and Technology (who have been spearheading the standardization of post-quantum cryptography) defines their post-quantum security levels is very telling. They define a specific security level by saying the scheme should be as hard to crack using either a classical or quantum computer as an existing symmetric cipher as follows:&lt;/p&gt;&lt;table&gt;&lt;tr&gt;&lt;td&gt;&lt;p&gt;&lt;b&gt;Level&lt;/b&gt;&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/td&gt;&lt;td&gt;&lt;p&gt;&lt;b&gt;Definition,&lt;/b&gt; as least as hard to break as â¦Â &lt;/p&gt;&lt;/td&gt;&lt;td&gt;&lt;p&gt;&lt;b&gt;Example&lt;/b&gt;&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/td&gt;&lt;/tr&gt;&lt;tr&gt;&lt;td&gt;&lt;p&gt;1&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/td&gt;&lt;td&gt;&lt;p&gt;To recover the key of &lt;b&gt;AES-128&lt;/b&gt; by exhaustive search&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/td&gt;&lt;td&gt;&lt;p&gt;ML-KEM-512, SLH-DSA-128s&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/td&gt;&lt;/tr&gt;&lt;tr&gt;&lt;td&gt;&lt;p&gt;2&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/td&gt;&lt;td&gt;&lt;p&gt;To find a collision in &lt;b&gt;SHA256&lt;/b&gt; by exhaustive search&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/td&gt;&lt;td&gt;&lt;p&gt;ML-DSA-44&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/td&gt;&lt;/tr&gt;&lt;tr&gt;&lt;td&gt;&lt;p&gt;3&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/td&gt;&lt;td&gt;&lt;p&gt;To recover the key of &lt;b&gt;AES-192&lt;/b&gt; by exhaustive search&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/td&gt;&lt;td&gt;&lt;p&gt;ML-KEM-768, ML-DSA-65&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/td&gt;&lt;/tr&gt;&lt;tr&gt;&lt;td&gt;&lt;p&gt;4&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/td&gt;&lt;td&gt;&lt;p&gt;To find a collision in &lt;b&gt;SHA384&lt;/b&gt; by exhaustive search&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/td&gt;&lt;td&gt;&lt;p&gt;&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/td&gt;&lt;/tr&gt;&lt;tr&gt;&lt;td&gt;&lt;p&gt;5&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/td&gt;&lt;td&gt;&lt;p&gt;To recover the key of &lt;b&gt;AES-256&lt;/b&gt; by exhaustive search&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/td&gt;&lt;td&gt;&lt;p&gt;ML-KEM-1024, SLH-DSA-256s, ML-DSA-87&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/td&gt;&lt;/tr&gt;&lt;/table&gt;&lt;p&gt;&lt;sup&gt;NIST PQC security levels, higher is harder to break (âmore secureâ). The examples ML-DSA, SLH-DSA and ML-KEM are covered below.&lt;/sup&gt;&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;There are good intentions behind suggesting doubling the key lengths of symmetric cryptography. In many use cases, the extra cost is not that high, and it mitigates any theoretical risk completely. Scaling symmetric cryptography is cheap: double the bits is typically far less than half the cost. So on the surface, it is simple advice.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;But if we insist on AES-256, it seems only logical to insist on NIST PQC level 5 for the public key cryptography as well. The problem is that public key cryptography does not scale very well. Depending on the scheme, going from level 1 to level 5 typically more than doubles data usage and CPU cost. As weâll see, deploying post-quantum signatures at level 1 is already painful, and deploying them at level 5 is debilitating.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;But more importantly, organizations only have limited resources. We wouldnât want an organization to prioritize upgrading AES-128 at the cost of leaving the definitely quantum-vulnerable RSA around.&lt;/p&gt;\n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h3 id=\&quot;first-migration-key-agreement\&quot;&gt;First migration: key agreement&lt;/h3&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#first-migration-key-agreement\&quot; aria-hidden=\&quot;true\&quot; class=\&quot;relative sm:absolute sm:-left-5\&quot;&gt;\n        &lt;svg width=\&quot;16\&quot; height=\&quot;16\&quot; viewBox=\&quot;0 0 24 24\&quot;&gt;&lt;path fill=\&quot;currentcolor\&quot; d=\&quot;m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z\&quot;&gt;&lt;/path&gt;&lt;/svg&gt;\n      &lt;/a&gt;\n    &lt;/div&gt;\n    &lt;p&gt;Symmetric ciphers are not enough on their own: how do I know which key to use when visiting a website for the first time? The browser canât just send a random key, as everyone listening in would see that key as well. Youâd think itâs impossible, but there is some clever math to solve this, so that the browser and server can agree on a shared key. Such a scheme is called a &lt;b&gt;key agreement &lt;/b&gt;mechanism, and is performed in the TLS &lt;a href=\&quot;https://www.cloudflare.com/learning/ssl/what-happens-in-a-tls-handshake/\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;handshake&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;. In 2024 almost all traffic is secured with &lt;a href=\&quot;https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Curve25519\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;X25519&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;, a DiffieâHellman-style key agreement, but its security is completely broken by &lt;a href=\&quot;https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shor%27s_algorithm\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;Shorâs algorithm&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; on a quantum computer. Thus, any communication secured today with DiffieâHellman, when stored, can be decrypted in the future by a quantum computer.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;This makes it &lt;b&gt;urgent&lt;/b&gt; to upgrade key agreement today. Luckily post-quantum key agreement is relatively straight-forward to deploy, and as we saw before, half the requests with Cloudflare end 2025 are already secured with post-quantum key agreement!&lt;/p&gt;\n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h3 id=\&quot;second-migration-signatures-certificates\&quot;&gt;Second migration: signatures / certificates&lt;/h3&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#second-migration-signatures-certificates\&quot; aria-hidden=\&quot;true\&quot; class=\&quot;relative sm:absolute sm:-left-5\&quot;&gt;\n        &lt;svg width=\&quot;16\&quot; height=\&quot;16\&quot; viewBox=\&quot;0 0 24 24\&quot;&gt;&lt;path fill=\&quot;currentcolor\&quot; d=\&quot;m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z\&quot;&gt;&lt;/path&gt;&lt;/svg&gt;\n      &lt;/a&gt;\n    &lt;/div&gt;\n    &lt;p&gt;The key agreement allows secure agreement on a key, but there is a big gap: we do not know &lt;i&gt;with whom&lt;/i&gt; we agreed on the key. If we only do key agreement, an attacker in the middle can do separate key agreements with the browser and server, and re-encrypt any exchanged messages. To prevent this we need one final ingredient: authentication.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;This is achieved using &lt;b&gt;signatures&lt;/b&gt;. When visiting a website, say &lt;a href=\&quot;https://cloudflare.com\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;cloudflare.com&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;, the web server presents a &lt;b&gt;certificate&lt;/b&gt; signed by a &lt;a href=\&quot;https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Certificate_authority\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;certification authority&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; (CA) that vouches that the public key in that certificate is controlled by &lt;a href=\&quot;https://cloudflare.com\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;cloudflare.com&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;. In turn, the web server signs the handshake and shared key using the private key corresponding to the public key in the certificate. This allows the client to be sure that theyâve done a key agreement with &lt;a href=\&quot;https://cloudflare.com\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;cloudflare.com&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;RSA and ECDSA are commonly used traditional signature schemes today. Again, Shorâs algorithm makes short work of them, allowing a quantum attacker to forge any signature. That means that an attacker with a quantum computer can impersonate (and &lt;a href=\&quot;https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Man-in-the-middle_attack\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;MitM&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;) any website for which we accept non post-quantum certificates.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;This attack can only be performed after quantum computers are able to crack RSA / ECDSA. This makes upgrading signature schemes for TLS on the face of it less urgent, as we only need to have everyone migrated before Q-day rolls around. Unfortunately, we will see that migration to post-quantum signatures is much &lt;b&gt;more difficult&lt;/b&gt;, and will require more time.&lt;/p&gt;\n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h2 id=\&quot;progress-timeline\&quot;&gt;Progress timeline&lt;/h2&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#progress-timeline\&quot; aria-hidden=\&quot;true\&quot; class=\&quot;relative sm:absolute sm:-left-5\&quot;&gt;\n        &lt;svg width=\&quot;16\&quot; height=\&quot;16\&quot; viewBox=\&quot;0 0 24 24\&quot;&gt;&lt;path fill=\&quot;currentcolor\&quot; d=\&quot;m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z\&quot;&gt;&lt;/path&gt;&lt;/svg&gt;\n      &lt;/a&gt;\n    &lt;/div&gt;\n    &lt;p&gt;Before we dive into the technical challenges of migrating the Internet to post-quantum cryptography, letâs have a look at how we got here, and what to expect in the coming years. Letâs start with how post-quantum cryptography came to be.&lt;/p&gt;\n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h3 id=\&quot;origin-of-post-quantum-cryptography\&quot;&gt;Origin of post-quantum cryptography&lt;/h3&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#origin-of-post-quantum-cryptography\&quot; aria-hidden=\&quot;true\&quot; class=\&quot;relative sm:absolute sm:-left-5\&quot;&gt;\n        &lt;svg width=\&quot;16\&quot; height=\&quot;16\&quot; viewBox=\&quot;0 0 24 24\&quot;&gt;&lt;path fill=\&quot;currentcolor\&quot; d=\&quot;m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z\&quot;&gt;&lt;/path&gt;&lt;/svg&gt;\n      &lt;/a&gt;\n    &lt;/div&gt;\n    &lt;p&gt;Physicists Feynman and Manin independently proposed quantum computers &lt;a href=\&quot;https://plato.stanford.edu/entries/qt-quantcomp/\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;around 1980&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;. It took another 14 years before Shor published &lt;a href=\&quot;https://ieeexplore.ieee.org/abstract/document/365700\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;his algorithm&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; attacking RSA / ECC. Most post-quantum cryptography predates Shorâs famous algorithm.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;There are various branches of post-quantum cryptography, of which the most prominent are lattice-based, hash-based, multivariate, code-based, and isogeny-based. Except for isogeny-based cryptography, none of these were initially conceived as post-quantum cryptography. In fact, early code-based and hash-based schemes are contemporaries of RSA, being proposed in the 1970s, and comfortably predate the publication of Shorâs algorithm in 1994. Also, the first multivariate scheme from 1988 is comfortably older than Shorâs algorithm. It is a nice coincidence that the most successful branch, lattice-based cryptography, is Shorâs closest contemporary, being proposed &lt;a href=\&quot;https://dl.acm.org/doi/pdf/10.1145/237814.237838\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;in 1996&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;. For comparison, elliptic curve cryptography, which is widely used today, was first proposed in 1985.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;In the years after the publication of Shorâs algorithm, cryptographers took measure of the existing cryptography: whatâs clearly broken, and what could be post-quantum secure? In 2006, the first annual &lt;a href=\&quot;https://postquantum.cr.yp.to/\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;International Workshop on Post-Quantum Cryptography&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; took place. From that conference, an introductory text &lt;a href=\&quot;https://www.researchgate.net/profile/Nicolas-Sendrier-2/publication/226115302_Code-Based_Cryptography/links/540d62d50cf2df04e7549388/Code-Based-Cryptography.pdf\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;was prepared&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;, which holds up rather well as an introduction to the field. A notable caveat is the &lt;a href=\&quot;https://eprint.iacr.org/2022/214.pdf\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;demise&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; of the &lt;a href=\&quot;https://www.pqcrainbow.org/\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;Rainbow&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; signature scheme. In that same year, 2006, the elliptic-curve key-agreement X25519 &lt;a href=\&quot;https://cr.yp.to/ecdh/curve25519-20060209.pdf\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;was proposed&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;, which now secures the majority of Internet connections, either on its own or as a hybrid with the post-quantum ML-KEM-768.Â &lt;/p&gt;\n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h2 id=\&quot;nist-completes-the-first-generation-of-pqc-standards\&quot;&gt;NIST completes the first generation of PQC standards&lt;/h2&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#nist-completes-the-first-generation-of-pqc-standards\&quot; aria-hidden=\&quot;true\&quot; class=\&quot;relative sm:absolute sm:-left-5\&quot;&gt;\n        &lt;svg width=\&quot;16\&quot; height=\&quot;16\&quot; viewBox=\&quot;0 0 24 24\&quot;&gt;&lt;path fill=\&quot;currentcolor\&quot; d=\&quot;m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z\&quot;&gt;&lt;/path&gt;&lt;/svg&gt;\n      &lt;/a&gt;\n    &lt;/div&gt;\n    &lt;p&gt;Ten years later, in 2016, &lt;a href=\&quot;https://nist.gov\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;NIST&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;, the US National Institute of Standards and Technology, &lt;a href=\&quot;https://csrc.nist.gov/CSRC/media/Projects/Post-Quantum-Cryptography/documents/call-for-proposals-final-dec-2016.pdf\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;launched a public competition&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; to standardize post-quantum cryptography. They used a similar open format as was used to standardize &lt;a href=\&quot;https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Advanced_Encryption_Standard\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;AES&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; in 2001, and &lt;a href=\&quot;https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NIST_hash_function_competition\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;SHA3&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; in 2012. Anyone can participate by submitting schemes and evaluating the proposals. Cryptographers from all over the world submitted algorithms. To focus attention, the list of submissions were whittled down over three rounds. From the original 82, based on public feedback, eight made it into the final round. From those eight, in 2022, NIST chose to &lt;a href=\&quot;https://blog.cloudflare.com/nist-post-quantum-surprise\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;pick four to standardize first&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;: one &lt;b&gt;KEM &lt;/b&gt;(for key agreement) and three signature schemes.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;table&gt;&lt;tr&gt;&lt;td&gt;&lt;p&gt;&lt;b&gt;Old name&lt;/b&gt;&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/td&gt;&lt;td&gt;&lt;p&gt;&lt;b&gt;New name&lt;/b&gt;&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/td&gt;&lt;td&gt;&lt;p&gt;&lt;b&gt;Branch&lt;/b&gt;&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/td&gt;&lt;/tr&gt;&lt;tr&gt;&lt;td&gt;&lt;p&gt;Kyber&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/td&gt;&lt;td&gt;&lt;p&gt;&lt;b&gt;ML-KEM&lt;/b&gt; (&lt;a href=\&quot;https://csrc.nist.gov/pubs/fips/203/final\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;FIPS 203&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;)\nModule-lattice based Key-Encapsulation Mechanism Standard&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/td&gt;&lt;td&gt;&lt;p&gt;Lattice-based&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/td&gt;&lt;/tr&gt;&lt;tr&gt;&lt;td&gt;&lt;p&gt;Dilithium&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/td&gt;&lt;td&gt;&lt;p&gt;&lt;b&gt;ML-DSA &lt;/b&gt;(&lt;a href=\&quot;https://csrc.nist.gov/pubs/fips/204/final\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;FIPS 204&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;)&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;Module-lattice based Digital Signature Standard&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/td&gt;&lt;td&gt;&lt;p&gt;Lattice-based&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/td&gt;&lt;/tr&gt;&lt;tr&gt;&lt;td&gt;&lt;p&gt;SPHINCS&lt;sup&gt;+&lt;/sup&gt;&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/td&gt;&lt;td&gt;&lt;p&gt;&lt;b&gt;SLH-DSA&lt;/b&gt; (&lt;a href=\&quot;https://csrc.nist.gov/pubs/fips/205/final\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;FIPS 205&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;)&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;Stateless Hash-Based Digital Signature Standard&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/td&gt;&lt;td&gt;&lt;p&gt;Hash-based&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/td&gt;&lt;/tr&gt;&lt;tr&gt;&lt;td&gt;&lt;p&gt;Falcon&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/td&gt;&lt;td&gt;&lt;p&gt;&lt;b&gt;FN-DSA &lt;/b&gt;(not standardised yet)&lt;b&gt;\n&lt;/b&gt;FFT over NTRU lattices Digital Signature Standard&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/td&gt;&lt;td&gt;&lt;p&gt;Lattice-based&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/td&gt;&lt;/tr&gt;&lt;/table&gt;&lt;p&gt;The final standards for the first three have been published August 2024. FN-DSA is late and weâll discuss that later.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;ML-KEM is the only post-quantum key agreement standardised now, and despite some occasional difficulty with its larger key sizes, itâs mostly a drop-in upgrade.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;The situation is rather different with the signatures: itâs quite telling that NIST chose to pursue standardising three already. And there are even more signatures set to be &lt;a href=\&quot;https://blog.cloudflare.com/another-look-at-pq-signatures/\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;standardized in the future&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;. The reason is that none of the proposed signatures are close to ideal. In short, they all have much larger keys and signatures than weâre used to.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;From a security standpoint SLH-DSA is the most conservative choice, but also the worst performer. For public key and signature sizes, FN-DSA is as good as it gets for these three, but it is difficult to implement signing safely because of floating-point arithmetic. Due to FN-DSAâs limited applicability and design complexity, NIST chose to focus on the other three schemes first.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;This leaves ML-DSA as the default pick. More in depth comparisons are included below.&lt;/p&gt;\n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h2 id=\&quot;adoption-of-pqc-in-protocol-standards\&quot;&gt;Adoption of PQC in protocol standards&lt;/h2&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#adoption-of-pqc-in-protocol-standards\&quot; aria-hidden=\&quot;true\&quot; class=\&quot;relative sm:absolute sm:-left-5\&quot;&gt;\n        &lt;svg width=\&quot;16\&quot; height=\&quot;16\&quot; viewBox=\&quot;0 0 24 24\&quot;&gt;&lt;path fill=\&quot;currentcolor\&quot; d=\&quot;m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z\&quot;&gt;&lt;/path&gt;&lt;/svg&gt;\n      &lt;/a&gt;\n    &lt;/div&gt;\n    &lt;p&gt;Having NISTâs standards is not enough. Itâs also required to standardize the way the new algorithms are used in higher level protocols. In many cases, such as key agreement in TLS, this can be as simple as assigning an identifier to the new algorithms. In other cases, such as &lt;a href=\&quot;https://www.cloudflare.com/dns/dnssec/how-dnssec-works/\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;DNSSEC&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;, it requires a bit more thought. Many working groups at the &lt;a href=\&quot;https://www.ietf.org/\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;IETF&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; have been preparing for years for the arrival of NISTâs final standards, and we expected many protocol integrations to be finalized soon after, before the end of 2024. That was too optimistic: some are done, but many are not finished yet.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;Letâs start with the good news and look at what is done.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;ul&gt;&lt;li&gt;&lt;p&gt;The hybrid TLS key agreement &lt;a href=\&quot;https://datatracker.ietf.org/doc/draft-ietf-tls-ecdhe-mlkem/\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;X25519MLKEM768&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; that combines X25519 and ML-KEM-768 (more about it later) is ready to use and is indeed quite widely deployed. Other protocols are likewise adopting ML-KEM in a hybrid mode of operation, such as &lt;a href=\&quot;https://datatracker.ietf.org/doc/draft-ietf-ipsecme-ikev2-mlkem/\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;IPsec&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;, which is ready to go for simple setups. (For certain setups, there is a &lt;a href=\&quot;https://datatracker.ietf.org/doc/draft-ietf-ipsecme-ikev2-downgrade-prevention/\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;litle wrinkle&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; that still needs to be figured out. Weâll cover that in a future blog post.)\n\nIt might be surprising that the corresponding RFCs have not been published yet. Registering a key agreement to TLS or IPsec does not require an RFC though. In both cases, the RFC is still being pursued to avoid confusion for those that would expect an RFC, and for TLS itâs required to mark the key agreement as recommended.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/li&gt;&lt;li&gt;&lt;p&gt;For signatures, ML-DSAâs integration &lt;a href=\&quot;https://datatracker.ietf.org/doc/draft-ietf-lamps-dilithium-certificates/\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;in X.509&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; certificates and &lt;a href=\&quot;https://datatracker.ietf.org/doc/draft-ietf-tls-mldsa/\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;TLS&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; are good to go. The former is a freshly minted RFC, and the latter doesnât require one.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/li&gt;&lt;/ul&gt;&lt;p&gt;Now, for the bad news. At the time of writing, October 2025, the IETF hasnât &lt;a href=\&quot;https://datatracker.ietf.org/doc/draft-ietf-lamps-pq-composite-sigs/\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;locked down&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; how to do hybrid certificates: certificates where both a post-quantum and a traditional signature scheme are combined. But itâs close. We hope thisâll be figured out early 2026.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;But if itâs just assigning some identifiers, whatâs the cause of the delay? Mostly itâs about choice. Letâs start with the choices that had to be made in ML-DSA.&lt;/p&gt;\n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h4 id=\&quot;ml-dsa-delays-much-ado-about-prehashing-and-private-key-formats\&quot;&gt;ML-DSA delays: much ado about prehashing and private key formats&lt;/h4&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#ml-dsa-delays-much-ado-about-prehashing-and-private-key-formats\&quot; aria-hidden=\&quot;true\&quot; class=\&quot;relative sm:absolute sm:-left-5\&quot;&gt;\n        &lt;svg width=\&quot;16\&quot; height=\&quot;16\&quot; viewBox=\&quot;0 0 24 24\&quot;&gt;&lt;path fill=\&quot;currentcolor\&quot; d=\&quot;m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z\&quot;&gt;&lt;/path&gt;&lt;/svg&gt;\n      &lt;/a&gt;\n    &lt;/div&gt;\n    &lt;p&gt;The two major topics of discussion for ML-DSA certificates were prehashing and the private key format.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;Prehashing is where one part of the system hashes the message, and another creates the final signatures. This is useful, if you donât want to send a big file to an &lt;a href=\&quot;https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hardware_security_module\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;HSM&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; to sign. Early drafts of ML-DSAÂ  support prehashing with SHAKE256, but that &lt;a href=\&quot;https://csrc.nist.gov/csrc/media/Projects/post-quantum-cryptography/documents/faq/fips204-sec6-03192025.pdf\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;was&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; not obvious. In the final version of ML-DSA, NIST included two variants: regular ML-DSA, and an explicitly prehashed version, where you are allowed to choose any hash. Having different variants is not ideal, as users will have to choose which one to pick; not all software might support all variants; and testing/validation has to be done for all. Itâs not controversial to want to pick just one variant, but the issue &lt;a href=\&quot;https://globalplatform.org/wp-content/uploads/2025/01/4_ML-DSA-and-ML-KEM-Landmines-1.pdf\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;is&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; &lt;a href=\&quot;https://keymaterial.net/2024/11/05/hashml-dsa-considered-harmful/\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;which&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;. After plenty of debate, regular ML-DSA was chosen.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;The second matter is &lt;a href=\&quot;https://datatracker.ietf.org/meeting/122/materials/slides-122-pquip-the-great-private-key-war-of-25-02.pdf\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;private key forma&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;t. Because of the way that candidates are compared on performance benchmarks, it looks good for the original ML-DSA submission to cache some computation in the private key. This means that the private key is larger (several kilobytes) than it needs to be and requires more validation steps. It was suggested to cut the private key down to its bare essentials: just a 32-byte &lt;i&gt;seed&lt;/i&gt;. For the final standard, NIST decided to allow both the seed and the original larger private key. This is not &lt;a href=\&quot;https://keymaterial.net/2025/02/19/how-not-to-format-a-private-key/\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;ideal&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;: better stick to one of the two. In this case, the IETF wasnât able to make a choice, and even added a third option: a pair of both the seed and expanded private key. Technically almost everyone agreed that &lt;i&gt;seed&lt;/i&gt; is the superior choice, but the reason it wasnât palatable is that some vendors already created keys for which they didnât keep the &lt;i&gt;seed&lt;/i&gt; around. Yes, we already have post-quantum legacy. It took almost a year to make these two choices.&lt;/p&gt;\n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h4 id=\&quot;hybrids-require-many-choices\&quot;&gt;Hybrids require many choices&lt;/h4&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#hybrids-require-many-choices\&quot; aria-hidden=\&quot;true\&quot; class=\&quot;relative sm:absolute sm:-left-5\&quot;&gt;\n        &lt;svg width=\&quot;16\&quot; height=\&quot;16\&quot; viewBox=\&quot;0 0 24 24\&quot;&gt;&lt;path fill=\&quot;currentcolor\&quot; d=\&quot;m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z\&quot;&gt;&lt;/path&gt;&lt;/svg&gt;\n      &lt;/a&gt;\n    &lt;/div&gt;\n    &lt;p&gt;To define an ML-DSA hybrid signature scheme, there are many more choices to make. With which traditional scheme to combine ML-DSA? What security levels on both sides. Then we also need to make choices for both schemes: which private key format to use? Which hash to use with ECDSA? Hybrids have new questions of their own. Do we allow reuse of the keys in the hybrid, and for that, do we want to prevent stripping attacks? Also, the question of prehashing returns with a third option: prehash on the hybrid level.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;The &lt;a href=\&quot;https://datatracker.ietf.org/doc/draft-ietf-lamps-pq-composite-sigs/12/\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;October 2025 draft&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; for ML-DSA hybrid signatures contains 18 variants, down from &lt;a href=\&quot;https://datatracker.ietf.org/doc/draft-ietf-lamps-pq-composite-sigs/03/\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;26&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; a year earlier. Again, everyone agrees that that is too much, but itâs been hard to whittle it down further. To help end-users choose, a short list was added, which started with three options, and of course grew itself to &lt;a href=\&quot;https://www.ietf.org/archive/id/draft-ietf-lamps-pq-composite-sigs-12.html#section-11.3\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;six&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;. Of those, we think MLDSA44-ECDSA-P256-SHA256 will see wide support and use on the Internet.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;Now, letâs return to key agreement for which the standards have been set.&lt;/p&gt;\n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h2 id=\&quot;tls-stacks-get-support-for-ml-kem\&quot;&gt;TLS stacks get support for ML-KEM&lt;/h2&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#tls-stacks-get-support-for-ml-kem\&quot; aria-hidden=\&quot;true\&quot; class=\&quot;relative sm:absolute sm:-left-5\&quot;&gt;\n        &lt;svg width=\&quot;16\&quot; height=\&quot;16\&quot; viewBox=\&quot;0 0 24 24\&quot;&gt;&lt;path fill=\&quot;currentcolor\&quot; d=\&quot;m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z\&quot;&gt;&lt;/path&gt;&lt;/svg&gt;\n      &lt;/a&gt;\n    &lt;/div&gt;\n    &lt;p&gt;The next step is software support. Not all ecosystems can move at the same speed, but weâve seen major adoption of post-quantum key agreement to counter store-now/decrypt-later already. Recent versions of all major browsers, and many TLS libraries and platforms, notably OpenSSL, Go, and recent Apple OSes have enabled X25519MLKEM768 by default. We keep an overview &lt;a href=\&quot;https://developers.cloudflare.com/ssl/post-quantum-cryptography/pqc-support/\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;here&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;Again, for TLS there is a big difference again between key agreement and signatures. For key agreement, the server and client can add and enable support for post-quantum key agreement independently. Once enabled on both sides, TLS negotiation will use post-quantum key agreement. We go into detail on TLS negotiation in &lt;a href=\&quot;https://blog.cloudflare.com/post-quantum-for-all#tls-anchor\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;this blog post&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;. If your product just uses TLS, your store-now/decrypt-now problem could be solved by a simple software update of the TLS library.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;Post-quantum TLS certificates are more of a hassle. Unless you control both ends, youâll need to install two certificates: one post-quantum certificate for the new clients, and a traditional one for the old clients. If you arenât using automated issuance of certificates yet, this might be a good reason to &lt;a href=\&quot;https://letsencrypt.org/docs/client-options/\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;check that out&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;. TLS allows the client to signal which signature schemes it supports so that the server can choose to serve a post-quantum certificate only to those clients that support it. Unfortunately, although almost all TLS libraries support setting up multiple certificates, not all servers expose that configuration. If they do, it will still require a configuration change in most cases. (Although undoubtedly &lt;a href=\&quot;https://caddyserver.com/\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;caddy&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; will do it for you.)&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;Talking about post-quantum certificates: it will take some time before Certification Authorities (CAs) can issue them. Their &lt;a href=\&quot;https://csrc.nist.gov/glossary/term/hardware_security_module_hsm\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;HSMs&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; will first need (hardware) support, which then will need to be audited. Also, the &lt;a href=\&quot;https://cabforum.org/\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;CA/Browser forum&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; needs to approve the use of the new algorithms. Root programs have different opinions about timelines. From the grapevine, we hear one of the root programs is preparing a pilot to accept one-year ML-DSA-87 certificates, perhaps even before the end of 2025. A CA/Browser forum ballot is &lt;a href=\&quot;https://github.com/cabforum/servercert/pull/624\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;being drafted&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; to support this. Chrome on the other hand, &lt;a href=\&quot;https://www.youtube.com/live/O_BXzJv16zQ?t=19274s\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;prefers&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; to solve the large certificate issue first. For the early movers, the audits are likely to be the bottleneck, as there will be a lot of submissions after the publication of the NIST standards. Although weâll see the first post-quantum certificates in 2026, itâs unlikely they will be broadly available or trusted by all browsers before 2027.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;We are in an interesting in-between time, where a lot of Internet traffic is protected by post-quantum key agreement, but not a single public post-quantum certificate is used.&lt;/p&gt;\n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h2 id=\&quot;the-search-continues-for-more-schemes\&quot;&gt;The search continues for more schemes&lt;/h2&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#the-search-continues-for-more-schemes\&quot; aria-hidden=\&quot;true\&quot; class=\&quot;relative sm:absolute sm:-left-5\&quot;&gt;\n        &lt;svg width=\&quot;16\&quot; height=\&quot;16\&quot; viewBox=\&quot;0 0 24 24\&quot;&gt;&lt;path fill=\&quot;currentcolor\&quot; d=\&quot;m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z\&quot;&gt;&lt;/path&gt;&lt;/svg&gt;\n      &lt;/a&gt;\n    &lt;/div&gt;\n    &lt;p&gt;NIST is not quite done standardizing post-quantum cryptography. There are two more post-quantum competitions running: &lt;b&gt;round 4&lt;/b&gt; and the &lt;b&gt;signatures onramp&lt;/b&gt;.&lt;/p&gt;\n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h3 id=\&quot;round-4-winner-hqc\&quot;&gt;Round 4 winner: HQC&lt;/h3&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#round-4-winner-hqc\&quot; aria-hidden=\&quot;true\&quot; class=\&quot;relative sm:absolute sm:-left-5\&quot;&gt;\n        &lt;svg width=\&quot;16\&quot; height=\&quot;16\&quot; viewBox=\&quot;0 0 24 24\&quot;&gt;&lt;path fill=\&quot;currentcolor\&quot; d=\&quot;m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z\&quot;&gt;&lt;/path&gt;&lt;/svg&gt;\n      &lt;/a&gt;\n    &lt;/div&gt;\n    &lt;p&gt;NIST only standardized one post-quantum key agreement so far: ML-KEM. Theyâd like to have a second one, a &lt;b&gt;backup KEM&lt;/b&gt;, not based on lattices in case those turn out to be weaker than expected. To find it,Â  they extended the original competition with a fourth round to pick a backup KEM among the finalists. In March 2025, &lt;a href=\&quot;https://www.nist.gov/news-events/news/2025/03/nist-selects-hqc-fifth-algorithm-post-quantum-encryption#:~:text=NIST%20has%20chosen%20a%20new,were%20discovered%20in%20ML%2DKEM.\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;HQC&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; was &lt;a href=\&quot;https://www.nist.gov/news-events/news/2025/03/nist-selects-hqc-fifth-algorithm-post-quantum-encryption#:~:text=NIST%20has%20chosen%20a%20new,were%20discovered%20in%20ML%2DKEM.\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;selected&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; to be standardized.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;HQC performs much worse than ML-KEM on every single metric. HQC-1, the lowest security level variant, requires 7kB of data on the wire. This is almost double the 3kB required for ML-KEM-1024, the highest security level variant. There is a similar gap in CPU performance. Also HQC scales worse with security level: where ML-KEM-1024 is about double the cost of ML-KEM-512, the highest security level of HQC requires three times the data (21kB!) and more than four times the compute.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;What about the security? To hedge against gradually improved attacks, ML-KEM-768 has a clear edge over HQC-1, it performs much better, and it has a huge security margin at level 3 compared to level 1. What about leaps? Both ML-KEM and HQC use a similar algebraic structure on top of plain lattices and codes respectively: it is not inconceivable that a breakthrough there could apply to both. Now, also without the algebraic structure, codes and lattices feel related. Weâre well into speculation: a catastrophic attack on lattices might not affect codes, but it wouldnât be surprising too if it did. After all, RSA and ECC that are more dissimilar are both broken by quantum computers.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;There might still be peace of mind to keep HQC around just in case. Here, weâd like to share an anecdote from the chaotic week when it was not clear yet that Chenâs quantum algorithm against lattices was flawed. What to replace ML-KEM with if it would be affected? HQC was briefly considered, but it was clear that an adjusted variant of ML-KEM would still be much more performant.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;Stepping back: that weâre looking for a &lt;i&gt;second&lt;/i&gt; efficient KEM is a luxury position. If I were granted a wish for a new post-quantum scheme, I wouldnât ask for a better KEM, but for a better signature scheme. Letâs see if I get lucky.&lt;/p&gt;\n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h3 id=\&quot;signatures-onramp\&quot;&gt;Signatures onramp&lt;/h3&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#signatures-onramp\&quot; aria-hidden=\&quot;true\&quot; class=\&quot;relative sm:absolute sm:-left-5\&quot;&gt;\n        &lt;svg width=\&quot;16\&quot; height=\&quot;16\&quot; viewBox=\&quot;0 0 24 24\&quot;&gt;&lt;path fill=\&quot;currentcolor\&quot; d=\&quot;m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z\&quot;&gt;&lt;/path&gt;&lt;/svg&gt;\n      &lt;/a&gt;\n    &lt;/div&gt;\n    &lt;p&gt;In late 2022, after announcing the first four picks, NIST also called a new competition, dubbed the &lt;i&gt;signatures onramp&lt;/i&gt;, to find &lt;a href=\&quot;https://csrc.nist.gov/projects/pqc-dig-sig\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;additional signature schemes&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;. The competition has two goals. The first is hedging against cryptanalytic breakthroughs against lattice-based cryptography. NIST would like to standardize a signature that performs better than SLH-DSA (both in size and compute), but is not based on lattices. Secondly, theyâre looking for a signature scheme that might do well in use cases where the current roster doesnât do well: we will discuss those at length later on in this post.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;In July 2023, NIST posted the &lt;a href=\&quot;https://csrc.nist.gov/news/2023/additional-pqc-digital-signature-candidates\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;40 submissions&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; they received for a first round of public review. The cryptographic community got to work, and as is quite normal for a first round, many of the schemes were broken within a week. By February 2024, ten submissions were broken completely, and several others were weakened drastically. Out of the standing candidates, in October 2024, NIST selected 14 submissions for the second round.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;A year ago, we wrote &lt;a href=\&quot;https://blog.cloudflare.com/another-look-at-pq-signatures/\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;a blog post&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; covering these 14 submissions in great detail. The short of it: there has been amazing progress on post-quantum signature schemes. We will touch briefly upon them later on, and give some updates on the advances since last year. It is worth mentioning that just like the main post-quantum competition, the selection process will take many years. It is unlikely that any of these onramp signature schemes will be standardized before 2028 â if theyâre not broken in the first place. That means that although theyâre very welcome in the future, we canât trust that better signature schemes will solve our problems today.
