As Eric Rescorla, the editor of TLS 1.3, &lt;a href=\&quot;https://educatedguesswork.org/posts/pq-emergency/\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;writes&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;: âYou go to war with the algorithms you have, not the ones you wish you had.â&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;With that in mind, let&amp;#39;s look at the progress of deployments.&lt;/p&gt;\n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h2 id=\&quot;migrating-the-internet-to-post-quantum-key-agreement\&quot;&gt;Migrating the Internet to post-quantum key agreement&lt;/h2&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#migrating-the-internet-to-post-quantum-key-agreement\&quot; aria-hidden=\&quot;true\&quot; class=\&quot;relative sm:absolute sm:-left-5\&quot;&gt;\n        &lt;svg width=\&quot;16\&quot; height=\&quot;16\&quot; viewBox=\&quot;0 0 24 24\&quot;&gt;&lt;path fill=\&quot;currentcolor\&quot; d=\&quot;m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z\&quot;&gt;&lt;/path&gt;&lt;/svg&gt;\n      &lt;/a&gt;\n    &lt;/div&gt;\n    &lt;p&gt;Now that we have the big picture, letâs dive into some finer details about this X25519MLKEM768 thatâs widely deployed now.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;First the post-quantum part. ML-KEM was submitted under the name &lt;a href=\&quot;https://pq-crystals.org/kyber/index.shtml\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;CRYSTALS-Kyber&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;. Even though itâs a US standard, its designers work in industry and academia across France, Switzerland, the Netherlands, Belgium, Germany, Canada, China, and the United States. Letâs have a look at its performance.&lt;/p&gt;\n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h2 id=\&quot;ml-kem-versus-x25519\&quot;&gt;ML-KEM versus X25519&lt;/h2&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#ml-kem-versus-x25519\&quot; aria-hidden=\&quot;true\&quot; class=\&quot;relative sm:absolute sm:-left-5\&quot;&gt;\n        &lt;svg width=\&quot;16\&quot; height=\&quot;16\&quot; viewBox=\&quot;0 0 24 24\&quot;&gt;&lt;path fill=\&quot;currentcolor\&quot; d=\&quot;m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z\&quot;&gt;&lt;/path&gt;&lt;/svg&gt;\n      &lt;/a&gt;\n    &lt;/div&gt;\n    &lt;p&gt;Today the vast majority of clients use the traditional key agreement X25519. Letâs compare that to ML-KEM.&lt;/p&gt;\n          &lt;figure class=\&quot;kg-card kg-image-card\&quot;&gt;\n          &lt;Image src=\&quot;https://cf-assets.www.cloudflare.com/zkvhlag99gkb/VCx6lbwzhKt4FywhRAZbk/4b7956adbb9a7690d3c3c6ce5d830fe1/Screenshot_2025-10-28_at_13.41.31.png\&quot; alt=\&quot;\&quot; class=\&quot;kg-image\&quot; width=\&quot;1364\&quot; height=\&quot;436\&quot; loading=\&quot;lazy\&quot;/&gt;\n          &lt;/figure&gt;&lt;p&gt;&lt;sup&gt;Size and CPU compared between X25519 and ML-KEM. Performance varies considerably by hardware platform and implementation constraints, and should be taken as a rough indication only.&lt;/sup&gt;&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;ML-KEM-512, -768 and -1024 aim to be as resistant to (quantum) attack as AES-128, -192 and -256 respectively. Even at the AES-128 level, ML-KEM is much bigger than X25519, requiring 800+768=1,568 bytes over the wire, whereas X25519 requires a mere 64 bytes.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;On the other hand, even ML-KEM-1024 is typically significantly faster than X25519, although this can vary quite a bit depending on your platform and implementation.&lt;/p&gt;\n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h2 id=\&quot;ml-kem-768-and-x25519\&quot;&gt;ML-KEM-768 and X25519&lt;/h2&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#ml-kem-768-and-x25519\&quot; aria-hidden=\&quot;true\&quot; class=\&quot;relative sm:absolute sm:-left-5\&quot;&gt;\n        &lt;svg width=\&quot;16\&quot; height=\&quot;16\&quot; viewBox=\&quot;0 0 24 24\&quot;&gt;&lt;path fill=\&quot;currentcolor\&quot; d=\&quot;m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z\&quot;&gt;&lt;/path&gt;&lt;/svg&gt;\n      &lt;/a&gt;\n    &lt;/div&gt;\n    &lt;p&gt;We are not taking advantage of that speed boost just yet. Like many other early adopters, we like to play it safe and deploy a &lt;b&gt;hybrid&lt;/b&gt; key-agreement &lt;a href=\&quot;https://datatracker.ietf.org/doc/draft-ietf-tls-ecdhe-mlkem/\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;combining&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; X25519 and ML-KEM-768. This combination might surprise you for two reasons.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;ol&gt;&lt;li&gt;&lt;p&gt;Why combine X25519 (â128 bits of securityâ) with ML-KEM-768 (â192 bits of securityâ)?&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/li&gt;&lt;li&gt;&lt;p&gt;Why bother with the non post-quantum X25519?&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/li&gt;&lt;/ol&gt;&lt;p&gt;The apparent security level mismatch is a hedge against improvements in cryptanalysis in lattice-based cryptography. There is a lot of trust in the (non post-quantum) security of X25519: matching AES-128 is more than enough. Although we are comfortable in the security of ML-KEM-512 today, over the coming decades cryptanalysis could improve. Thus, weâd like to keep a margin for now.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;The inclusion of X25519 has two reasons. First, there is always a remote chance that a breakthrough renders all variants of ML-KEM insecure. In that case, X25519 still provides non-post-quantum security, and our post-quantum migration didnât make things worse.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;More important is that we do not only worry about attacks on the algorithm, but also on the implementation. A noteworthy example where we dodged a bullet is that of &lt;a href=\&quot;https://kyberslash.cr.yp.to/\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;KyberSlash&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;, a timing attack that affected many implementations of Kyber (an earlier version of ML-KEM), including &lt;a href=\&quot;https://github.com/cloudflare/circl/security/advisories/GHSA-9763-4f94-gfch\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;our own&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;. Luckily KyberSlash does not affect Kyber as it is used in TLS. A similar implementation mistake that would actually affect TLS, is likely to require an active attacker. In that case, the likely aim of the attacker wouldnât be to decrypt data decades down the line, but steal a cookie or other token, or inject a payload. Including X25519 prevents such an attack.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;So how well do ML-KEM-768 and X25519 together perform in practice?&lt;/p&gt;\n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h2 id=\&quot;performance-and-protocol-ossification\&quot;&gt;Performance and protocol ossification&lt;/h2&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#performance-and-protocol-ossification\&quot; aria-hidden=\&quot;true\&quot; class=\&quot;relative sm:absolute sm:-left-5\&quot;&gt;\n        &lt;svg width=\&quot;16\&quot; height=\&quot;16\&quot; viewBox=\&quot;0 0 24 24\&quot;&gt;&lt;path fill=\&quot;currentcolor\&quot; d=\&quot;m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z\&quot;&gt;&lt;/path&gt;&lt;/svg&gt;\n      &lt;/a&gt;\n    &lt;/div&gt;\n    \n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h3 id=\&quot;browser-experiments\&quot;&gt;Browser experiments&lt;/h3&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#browser-experiments\&quot; aria-hidden=\&quot;true\&quot; class=\&quot;relative sm:absolute sm:-left-5\&quot;&gt;\n        &lt;svg width=\&quot;16\&quot; height=\&quot;16\&quot; viewBox=\&quot;0 0 24 24\&quot;&gt;&lt;path fill=\&quot;currentcolor\&quot; d=\&quot;m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z\&quot;&gt;&lt;/path&gt;&lt;/svg&gt;\n      &lt;/a&gt;\n    &lt;/div&gt;\n    &lt;p&gt;Being well aware of potential compatibility and performance issues, Google started &lt;a href=\&quot;https://security.googleblog.com/2016/07/experimenting-with-post-quantum.html\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;a first experiment&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; with post-quantum cryptography back in 2016, the same year NIST started their competition. This was followed up by a second larger joint experiment by &lt;a href=\&quot;https://blog.cloudflare.com/towards-post-quantum-cryptography-in-tls/\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;Cloudflare&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; and &lt;a href=\&quot;https://www.imperialviolet.org/2018/12/12/cecpq2.html\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;Google&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; in 2018. We tested two different hybrid post-quantum key agreements: CECPQ2, which is a combination of the lattice-based NTRU-HRSS and X25519, and CECPQ2b, a combination of the isogeny-based SIKE and again X25519. NTRU-HRSS is very similar to ML-KEM in size, but is computationally somewhat more taxing on the client-side. SIKE on the other hand, has very small keys, is computationally very expensive, and was &lt;a href=\&quot;https://eprint.iacr.org/2022/975.pdf\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;completely broken&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; in 2022. With respect to TLS handshake times, X25519+NTRU-HRSS performed very well.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;Unfortunately, a small but significant fraction of clients experienced broken connections with NTRU-HRSS. The reason: the size of the NTRU-HRSS keyshares. In the past, when creating a TLS connection, the first message sent by the client, the so-called &lt;i&gt;ClientHello&lt;/i&gt;, almost always fit within a single network packet. The TLS specification allows for a larger &lt;i&gt;ClientHello&lt;/i&gt;, however no one really made use of that. Thus, protocol ossification strikes again as there are some middleboxes, load-balancers, and other software that tacitly assume the &lt;i&gt;ClientHello&lt;/i&gt; always fits in a single packet.&lt;/p&gt;\n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h2 id=\&quot;long-road-to-50\&quot;&gt;Long road to 50%&lt;/h2&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#long-road-to-50\&quot; aria-hidden=\&quot;true\&quot; class=\&quot;relative sm:absolute sm:-left-5\&quot;&gt;\n        &lt;svg width=\&quot;16\&quot; height=\&quot;16\&quot; viewBox=\&quot;0 0 24 24\&quot;&gt;&lt;path fill=\&quot;currentcolor\&quot; d=\&quot;m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z\&quot;&gt;&lt;/path&gt;&lt;/svg&gt;\n      &lt;/a&gt;\n    &lt;/div&gt;\n    &lt;p&gt;Over the subsequent years, we kept experimenting with PQ, switching to Kyber in 2022, and ML-KEM in 2024. Chrome did a great job reaching out to vendors whose products were &lt;a href=\&quot;https://tldr.fail/\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;incompatible&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;. If it were not for these compatibility issues, we wouldâve likely seen Chrome ramp up post-quantum key agreement five years earlier. It took until March 2024 before Chrome felt comfortable enough to enable post-quantum key agreement by default on Desktop. After that many other clients, and all major browsers, have joined Chrome in enabling post-quantum key agreement by default. An incomplete timeline:&lt;/p&gt;&lt;table&gt;&lt;tr&gt;&lt;td&gt;&lt;p&gt;July 2016&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/td&gt;&lt;td&gt;&lt;p&gt;Chromeâs &lt;a href=\&quot;https://security.googleblog.com/2016/07/experimenting-with-post-quantum.html\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;first experiment with PQ&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; (CECPQ)&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/td&gt;&lt;/tr&gt;&lt;tr&gt;&lt;td&gt;&lt;p&gt;June 2018&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/td&gt;&lt;td&gt;&lt;p&gt;&lt;a href=\&quot;https://blog.cloudflare.com/the-tls-post-quantum-experiment/\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;Cloudflare&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; / &lt;a href=\&quot;https://www.imperialviolet.org/2018/12/12/cecpq2.html\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;Google&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; experiment (CECPQ2)&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/td&gt;&lt;/tr&gt;&lt;tr&gt;&lt;td&gt;&lt;p&gt;October 2022&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/td&gt;&lt;td&gt;&lt;p&gt;Cloudflare &lt;a href=\&quot;https://blog.cloudflare.com/post-quantum-for-all/\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;enables&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; PQ by default server side&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/td&gt;&lt;/tr&gt;&lt;tr&gt;&lt;td&gt;&lt;p&gt;November 2023&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/td&gt;&lt;td&gt;&lt;p&gt;Chrome ramps up PQ to 10% on Desktop&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/td&gt;&lt;/tr&gt;&lt;tr&gt;&lt;td&gt;&lt;p&gt;March 2024&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/td&gt;&lt;td&gt;&lt;p&gt;Chrome &lt;a href=\&quot;https://blog.chromium.org/2024/05/advancing-our-amazing-bet-on-asymmetric.html\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;enables&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; PQ by default on Desktop&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/td&gt;&lt;/tr&gt;&lt;tr&gt;&lt;td&gt;&lt;p&gt;August 2024&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/td&gt;&lt;td&gt;&lt;p&gt;Go &lt;a href=\&quot;https://github.com/golang/go/issues/67061\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;enables&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; PQ by default&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/td&gt;&lt;/tr&gt;&lt;tr&gt;&lt;td&gt;&lt;p&gt;November 2024&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/td&gt;&lt;td&gt;&lt;p&gt;Chrome enables PQ by default on Android and Firefox on Desktop.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/td&gt;&lt;/tr&gt;&lt;tr&gt;&lt;td&gt;&lt;p&gt;April 2025&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/td&gt;&lt;td&gt;&lt;p&gt;&lt;a href=\&quot;https://openssl-library.org/post/2025-04-08-openssl-35-final-release/\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;OpenSSL&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; enables PQ by default&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/td&gt;&lt;/tr&gt;&lt;tr&gt;&lt;td&gt;&lt;p&gt;October 2025&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/td&gt;&lt;td&gt;&lt;p&gt;Apple is &lt;a href=\&quot;https://support.apple.com/en-us/122756\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;rolling out&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; PQ by default with the release of iOS / iPadOS / macOS 26.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/td&gt;&lt;/tr&gt;&lt;/table&gt;&lt;p&gt;Itâs noteworthy that there is a gap between Chrome enabling PQ on Desktop and on Android. Although ML-KEM doesnât have a large performance impact, as seen in the graphs, itâs certainly not negligible, especially on the long tail of slower connections more prevalent on mobile, and it required more consideration to proceed.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;But weâre finally here now: over 50% (and rising!) of human traffic is protected against store-now/decrypt-later, making post-quantum key agreement the new security baseline for the Web.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;Browsers are one side of the equation, what about servers?&lt;/p&gt;\n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h3 id=\&quot;server-side-support\&quot;&gt;Server-side support&lt;/h3&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#server-side-support\&quot; aria-hidden=\&quot;true\&quot; class=\&quot;relative sm:absolute sm:-left-5\&quot;&gt;\n        &lt;svg width=\&quot;16\&quot; height=\&quot;16\&quot; viewBox=\&quot;0 0 24 24\&quot;&gt;&lt;path fill=\&quot;currentcolor\&quot; d=\&quot;m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z\&quot;&gt;&lt;/path&gt;&lt;/svg&gt;\n      &lt;/a&gt;\n    &lt;/div&gt;\n    &lt;p&gt;Back in 2022 we &lt;a href=\&quot;https://blog.cloudflare.com/post-quantum-for-all/\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;enabled&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; post-quantum key agreement server side for basically all customers. Google did the same for most of their servers (except GCP) in 2023. Since then many have followed. Jan Schaumann has been posting regular scans of the top 100k domains. In his September 2025 post, &lt;a href=\&quot;https://www.netmeister.org/blog/pqc-use-2025-09.html\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;he reports&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; 39% support PQ now, up from 28% only six months earlier. In his survey, we see not only support rolling out on large service providers, such as Amazon, Fastly, Squarespace, Google, and Microsoft, but also a trickle of self-hosted servers adding support hosted at Hetzner and OVHcloud.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;This is the publicly accessible web. What about servers behind a service like Cloudflare?&lt;/p&gt;\n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h3 id=\&quot;support-at-origins\&quot;&gt;Support at origins&lt;/h3&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#support-at-origins\&quot; aria-hidden=\&quot;true\&quot; class=\&quot;relative sm:absolute sm:-left-5\&quot;&gt;\n        &lt;svg width=\&quot;16\&quot; height=\&quot;16\&quot; viewBox=\&quot;0 0 24 24\&quot;&gt;&lt;path fill=\&quot;currentcolor\&quot; d=\&quot;m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z\&quot;&gt;&lt;/path&gt;&lt;/svg&gt;\n      &lt;/a&gt;\n    &lt;/div&gt;\n    &lt;p&gt;In &lt;a href=\&quot;https://blog.cloudflare.com/post-quantum-to-origins\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;September 2023&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;, we added support for our customers to enable post-quantum key agreement on connections from Cloudflare to their origins. Thatâs connection (3) in the following diagram:&lt;/p&gt;\n          &lt;figure class=\&quot;kg-card kg-image-card\&quot;&gt;\n          &lt;Image src=\&quot;https://cf-assets.www.cloudflare.com/zkvhlag99gkb/7dRJxj1f2otMM41sEKFoFG/d722378a6f74c4033787897334bb4e7a/image12.png\&quot; alt=\&quot;Typical connection flow when a visitor requests an uncached page. \&quot; class=\&quot;kg-image\&quot; width=\&quot;1600\&quot; height=\&quot;417\&quot; loading=\&quot;lazy\&quot;/&gt;\n          &lt;/figure&gt;&lt;p&gt;&lt;sup&gt;Typical connection flow when a visitor requests an uncached page.&lt;/sup&gt;&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;Back in 2023 only 0.5% of origins supported post-quantum key agreement. Through 2024 that hasnât changed much. This year, in 2025, we see support slowly pick up with software support rolling out, and weâre now at 3.7%.&lt;/p&gt;\n          &lt;figure class=\&quot;kg-card kg-image-card\&quot;&gt;\n          &lt;Image src=\&quot;https://cf-assets.www.cloudflare.com/zkvhlag99gkb/6LaKKWKWTli5NETFHlQ1za/e9eb1e750a72e62bdc522207451e7085/image7.png\&quot; alt=\&quot;Fraction of origins that support the post-quantum key agreement X25519MLKEM768.\&quot; class=\&quot;kg-image\&quot; width=\&quot;1999\&quot; height=\&quot;872\&quot; loading=\&quot;lazy\&quot;/&gt;\n          &lt;/figure&gt;&lt;p&gt;&lt;sup&gt;Fraction of origins that support the post-quantum key agreement X25519MLKEM768.&lt;/sup&gt;&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;3.7% doesnât sound impressive at all compared to the previous 50% and 39% for clients and public servers respectively, but itâs nothing to scoff at. There is much more diversity in origins than there are in clients: many more people have to do something to make that number move up. But itâs still a more than seven-fold increase, and letâs not forget that back in 2024 we celebrated reaching 1.8% of client support.For customers, origins arenât always easy to upgrade at all. Does that mean missing out on post-quantum security? No, not necessarily: you can secure the connection between Cloudflare and your origin by setting up &lt;a href=\&quot;https://developers.cloudflare.com/cloudflare-one/connections/connect-networks/\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;Cloudflare Tunnel&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; as a sidecar to your origin.&lt;/p&gt;\n          &lt;figure class=\&quot;kg-card kg-image-card\&quot;&gt;\n          &lt;Image src=\&quot;https://cf-assets.www.cloudflare.com/zkvhlag99gkb/3bfxtdySAPtc9hn9Qroztz/8233f1fecbed214b9584af6648488587/image3.png\&quot; alt=\&quot;\&quot; class=\&quot;kg-image\&quot; width=\&quot;1548\&quot; height=\&quot;525\&quot; loading=\&quot;lazy\&quot;/&gt;\n          &lt;/figure&gt;\n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h3 id=\&quot;ossification\&quot;&gt;Ossification&lt;/h3&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#ossification\&quot; aria-hidden=\&quot;true\&quot; class=\&quot;relative sm:absolute sm:-left-5\&quot;&gt;\n        &lt;svg width=\&quot;16\&quot; height=\&quot;16\&quot; viewBox=\&quot;0 0 24 24\&quot;&gt;&lt;path fill=\&quot;currentcolor\&quot; d=\&quot;m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z\&quot;&gt;&lt;/path&gt;&lt;/svg&gt;\n      &lt;/a&gt;\n    &lt;/div&gt;\n    &lt;p&gt;Support is all well and good, but as we saw with browser experiments, protocol ossification is a big concern. What does it look like with origins? Well, it depends.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;There are two ways to enable post-quantum key agreement: the fast way, and the slow but safer way. In both cases, if the origin doesnât support post-quantum, theyâll fall back safely to traditional key agreement. We explain the details in this &lt;a href=\&quot;https://blog.cloudflare.com/post-quantum-to-origins\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;blog post&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;, but in short, in the fast way we send the post-quantum keys immediately, and in the safer way we postpone it by one roundtrip using &lt;i&gt;HelloRetryRequest&lt;/i&gt;. All major browsers use the fast way.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;We have been regularly scanning all origins to see what they support. The good news is that all origins supported the safe but slow method. The fast method didnât fare as well, as we found that 0.05% of connections would break. Thatâs too high to enable the fast method by default. We did enable PQ to origins using the safer method by default for all non-enterprise customers and enterprise customers can opt in.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;We are not satisfied though until itâs fast and enabled for everyone. Thatâs why weâll &lt;a href=\&quot;https://blog.cloudflare.com/automatically-secure/#post-quantum-era\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;automatically enable&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; post-quantum to origins using the fast method for all customers, if our scans show itâs safe.&lt;/p&gt;\n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h3 id=\&quot;internal-connections\&quot;&gt;Internal connections&lt;/h3&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#internal-connections\&quot; aria-hidden=\&quot;true\&quot; class=\&quot;relative sm:absolute sm:-left-5\&quot;&gt;\n        &lt;svg width=\&quot;16\&quot; height=\&quot;16\&quot; viewBox=\&quot;0 0 24 24\&quot;&gt;&lt;path fill=\&quot;currentcolor\&quot; d=\&quot;m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z\&quot;&gt;&lt;/path&gt;&lt;/svg&gt;\n      &lt;/a&gt;\n    &lt;/div&gt;\n    &lt;p&gt;So far all the connections weâve been talking about are between Cloudflare and external parties. There are also a lot of internal connections within Cloudflare (marked 2 in the two diagrams above.) In 2023 we &lt;a href=\&quot;https://blog.cloudflare.com/post-quantum-cryptography-ga/\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;made a big push&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; to upgrade our internal connections to post-quantum key agreement. Compared to all the other post-quantum efforts we pursue, this has been, by far, the biggest job: we asked every engineering team in the company to stop what theyâre doing; take stock of the data and connections that their products secure; and upgrade them to post-quantum key agreement. In most cases the upgrade was simple. In fact, many teams were already upgraded by pulling in software updates. Still, figuring out that youâre already done can take quite some time! On a positive note, we didnât see any performance or ossification issues in this push.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;We have upgraded the majority of internal connections, but a long tail remains, which we continue to work on. The most important connection that we didnât get to upgrade in 2023 is the connection between WARP client and Cloudflare. In September 2025 we &lt;a href=\&quot;https://blog.cloudflare.com/post-quantum-warp/\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;upgraded it&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;, by moving from Wireguard to QUIC.&lt;/p&gt;\n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h2 id=\&quot;outlook\&quot;&gt;Outlook&lt;/h2&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#outlook\&quot; aria-hidden=\&quot;true\&quot; class=\&quot;relative sm:absolute sm:-left-5\&quot;&gt;\n        &lt;svg width=\&quot;16\&quot; height=\&quot;16\&quot; viewBox=\&quot;0 0 24 24\&quot;&gt;&lt;path fill=\&quot;currentcolor\&quot; d=\&quot;m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z\&quot;&gt;&lt;/path&gt;&lt;/svg&gt;\n      &lt;/a&gt;\n    &lt;/div&gt;\n    &lt;p&gt;As weâve seen, post-quantum key agreement, despite initial trouble with protocol ossification, has been straightforward to deploy. In the vast majority of cases itâs an uneventful software update. And with 50% deployment (and rising), itâs the new security baseline for the Internet.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;Letâs turn to the second, more difficult migration.&lt;/p&gt;\n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h2 id=\&quot;migrating-the-internet-to-post-quantum-signatures\&quot;&gt;Migrating the Internet to post-quantum signatures&lt;/h2&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#migrating-the-internet-to-post-quantum-signatures\&quot; aria-hidden=\&quot;true\&quot; class=\&quot;relative sm:absolute sm:-left-5\&quot;&gt;\n        &lt;svg width=\&quot;16\&quot; height=\&quot;16\&quot; viewBox=\&quot;0 0 24 24\&quot;&gt;&lt;path fill=\&quot;currentcolor\&quot; d=\&quot;m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z\&quot;&gt;&lt;/path&gt;&lt;/svg&gt;\n      &lt;/a&gt;\n    &lt;/div&gt;\n    &lt;p&gt;Now, weâll turn our attention to upgrading the signatures used on the Internet.&lt;/p&gt;\n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h2 id=\&quot;the-zoo-of-post-quantum-signatures\&quot;&gt;The zoo of post-quantum signatures&lt;/h2&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#the-zoo-of-post-quantum-signatures\&quot; aria-hidden=\&quot;true\&quot; class=\&quot;relative sm:absolute sm:-left-5\&quot;&gt;\n        &lt;svg width=\&quot;16\&quot; height=\&quot;16\&quot; viewBox=\&quot;0 0 24 24\&quot;&gt;&lt;path fill=\&quot;currentcolor\&quot; d=\&quot;m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z\&quot;&gt;&lt;/path&gt;&lt;/svg&gt;\n      &lt;/a&gt;\n    &lt;/div&gt;\n    &lt;p&gt;We wrote a &lt;a href=\&quot;https://blog.cloudflare.com/another-look-at-pq-signatures/\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;long deep dive&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; in the field of post-quantum signature schemes last year, November 2024. Most of that is still up-to-date, but there have been some exciting developments. Here weâll just go over some highlights and some exciting updates of last year.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;Letâs start by sizing up the post-quantum signatures we have available today at the AES-128 security level: ML-DSA-44 and the two variants of SLH-DSA. We use ML-DSA-44 as the baseline, as thatâs the scheme thatâs going to see the most widespread use initially. As a comparison, we also include the venerable Ed25519 and RSA-2048 in wide use today, as well as FN-DSA-512 which will be standardised soon and a sample of nine for TLS promising signature schemes from the signatures onramp.&lt;/p&gt;\n          &lt;figure class=\&quot;kg-card kg-image-card\&quot;&gt;\n          &lt;Image src=\&quot;https://cf-assets.www.cloudflare.com/zkvhlag99gkb/4NC2lO6hXKEFOgaVgQ7ExO/a0a65ddbb24d11ad96405f19aa344f4b/Screenshot_2025-10-28_at_13.18.54.png\&quot; alt=\&quot;\&quot; class=\&quot;kg-image\&quot; width=\&quot;1594\&quot; height=\&quot;1138\&quot; loading=\&quot;lazy\&quot;/&gt;\n          &lt;/figure&gt;&lt;p&gt;&lt;sup&gt;Comparison of various signature schemes at the security level of AES-128. CPU times vary significantly by platform and implementation constraints and should be taken as a rough indication only. â ï¸ FN-DSA signing time when using fast but dangerous floating-point arithmetic â see warning below. â ï¸ SQISign signing is not timing side-channel secure.&lt;/sup&gt;&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;It is immediately clear that none of the post-quantum signature schemes comes even close to being a drop-in replacement for Ed25519 (which is comparable to ECDSA P-256) as most of the signatures are simply much bigger. The exceptions are SQISign, MAYO, SNOVA, and UOV from the onramp, but theyâre far from ideal. MAYO, SNOVA, and UOV have large public keys, and SQISign requires a great amount of computation.&lt;/p&gt;\n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h3 id=\&quot;be-careful-with-fn-dsa\&quot;&gt;Be careful with FN-DSA&lt;/h3&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#be-careful-with-fn-dsa\&quot; aria-hidden=\&quot;true\&quot; class=\&quot;relative sm:absolute sm:-left-5\&quot;&gt;\n        &lt;svg width=\&quot;16\&quot; height=\&quot;16\&quot; viewBox=\&quot;0 0 24 24\&quot;&gt;&lt;path fill=\&quot;currentcolor\&quot; d=\&quot;m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z\&quot;&gt;&lt;/path&gt;&lt;/svg&gt;\n      &lt;/a&gt;\n    &lt;/div&gt;\n    &lt;p&gt;Looking ahead a bit: the best of the first competition seems to be FN-DSA-512. FN-DSA-512âs signatures and public key together are &lt;i&gt;only&lt;/i&gt; 1,563 bytes, with somewhat reasonable signing time. FN-DSA has an &lt;b&gt;achilles heel&lt;/b&gt; though â for acceptable signing performance, it requires fast floating-point arithmetic. Without it, signing is about 20 times slower. But speed is not enough, as the floating-point arithmetic has to run in constant time â without it, the FN-DSA private key can be recovered by timing signature creation. Writing safe FN-DSA implementations has turned out to be quite challenging, which makes FN-DSA dangerous when signatures are generated on the fly, such as in a TLS handshake. It is good to stress that this only affects signing. FN-DSA verification does not require floating-point arithmetic (and during verification there wouldnât be a private key to leak anyway.)&lt;/p&gt;\n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h2 id=\&quot;there-are-many-signatures-on-the-web\&quot;&gt;There are many signatures on the web&lt;/h2&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#there-are-many-signatures-on-the-web\&quot; aria-hidden=\&quot;true\&quot; class=\&quot;relative sm:absolute sm:-left-5\&quot;&gt;\n        &lt;svg width=\&quot;16\&quot; height=\&quot;16\&quot; viewBox=\&quot;0 0 24 24\&quot;&gt;&lt;path fill=\&quot;currentcolor\&quot; d=\&quot;m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z\&quot;&gt;&lt;/path&gt;&lt;/svg&gt;\n      &lt;/a&gt;\n    &lt;/div&gt;\n    &lt;p&gt;The biggest pain-point of migrating the Internet to post-quantum signatures, is that there are a lot of signatures even in a single connection. When you visit this very website for the first time, we send &lt;b&gt;five signatures and two public keys&lt;/b&gt;.&lt;/p&gt;\n          &lt;figure class=\&quot;kg-card kg-image-card\&quot;&gt;\n          &lt;Image src=\&quot;https://cf-assets.www.cloudflare.com/zkvhlag99gkb/6frWEoCLnBEZ5qztV8XoT4/25bd315190d8914f42f282679d6f525a/image9.png\&quot; alt=\&quot;\&quot; class=\&quot;kg-image\&quot; width=\&quot;1164\&quot; height=\&quot;1268\&quot; loading=\&quot;lazy\&quot;/&gt;\n          &lt;/figure&gt;&lt;p&gt;The majority of these are for the &lt;b&gt;certificate chain&lt;/b&gt;: the CA signs the intermediate certificate, which signs the leaf certificate, which in turn signs the TLS transcript to prove the authenticity of the server. If youâre keeping count: weâre still two signatures short.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;These are for &lt;b&gt;SCTs&lt;/b&gt; required for &lt;a href=\&quot;https://certificate.transparency.dev/howctworks/\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;certificate transparency&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;. Certificate transparency (CT) is a key, but lesser known, part of the &lt;a href=\&quot;https://smallstep.com/blog/everything-pki/#web-pki-vs-internal-pki\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;Web PKI&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;, the ecosystem that secures browser connections. Its goal is to publicly log every certificate issued, so that misissuances can be detected after the fact. Itâs the system thatâs behind &lt;a href=\&quot;http://crt.sh\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;crt.sh&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; and &lt;a href=\&quot;https://blog.cloudflare.com/new-regional-internet-traffic-and-certificate-transparency-insights-on-radar/\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;Cloudflare Radar&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;. CT has shown its value once more very recently by surfacing a &lt;a href=\&quot;https://blog.cloudflare.com/unauthorized-issuance-of-certificates-for-1-1-1-1/\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;rogue certificate for 1.1.1.1&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;Certificate transparency works by having independent parties run &lt;i&gt;CT logs&lt;/i&gt;. Before issuing a certificate, a CA must first submit it to at least two different CT logs. An SCT is a signature of a CT log that acts as a proof, a &lt;i&gt;receipt&lt;/i&gt;, that the certificate has been logged.&lt;/p&gt;\n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h3 id=\&quot;tailoring-signature-schemes\&quot;&gt;Tailoring signature schemes&lt;/h3&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#tailoring-signature-schemes\&quot; aria-hidden=\&quot;true\&quot; class=\&quot;relative sm:absolute sm:-left-5\&quot;&gt;\n        &lt;svg width=\&quot;16\&quot; height=\&quot;16\&quot; viewBox=\&quot;0 0 24 24\&quot;&gt;&lt;path fill=\&quot;currentcolor\&quot; d=\&quot;m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z\&quot;&gt;&lt;/path&gt;&lt;/svg&gt;\n      &lt;/a&gt;\n    &lt;/div&gt;\n    &lt;p&gt;There are two aspects of how a signature can be used that are worthwhile to highlight: whether the &lt;b&gt;public key is included&lt;/b&gt; with the signature, and whether the signature is &lt;b&gt;online&lt;/b&gt; or &lt;b&gt;offline&lt;/b&gt;.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;For the SCTs and the signature of the root on the intermediate, the public key is not transmitted during the handshake. Thus, for those, a signature scheme with smaller signatures but larger public keys, such as MAYO, SNOVA, or UOV, would be particularly well-suited. For the other signatures, the public key is included, and itâs more important to minimize the sizes of the combined public key and signature.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;The handshake signature is the only signature that is created online â all the other signatures are created ahead of time.Â  The handshake signature is created and verified only once, whereas the other signatures are typically verified many times by different clients. This means that for the handshake signature, itâs advantageous to balance signing and verification time which are both in the &lt;i&gt;hot path&lt;/i&gt;, whereas for the other signatures having better verification time at the cost of slower signing is worthwhile. This is one of the advantages RSA still enjoys over elliptic curve signatures today.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;Putting together different signature schemes is a fun puzzle, but it also comes with some drawbacks. Using multiple different schemes increases the attack surface because an algorithmic or implementation vulnerability in one compromises the whole. Also, the whole ecosystem needs to implement and optimize multiple algorithms, which is a significant burden.&lt;/p&gt;\n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h2 id=\&quot;putting-it-together\&quot;&gt;Putting it together&lt;/h2&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#putting-it-together\&quot; aria-hidden=\&quot;true\&quot; class=\&quot;relative sm:absolute sm:-left-5\&quot;&gt;\n        &lt;svg width=\&quot;16\&quot; height=\&quot;16\&quot; viewBox=\&quot;0 0 24 24\&quot;&gt;&lt;path fill=\&quot;currentcolor\&quot; d=\&quot;m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z\&quot;&gt;&lt;/path&gt;&lt;/svg&gt;\n      &lt;/a&gt;\n    &lt;/div&gt;\n    &lt;p&gt;So, what are some reasonable combinations to try?&lt;/p&gt;\n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h3 id=\&quot;with-nists-current-picks\&quot;&gt;With NISTâs current picks&lt;/h3&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#with-nists-current-picks\&quot; aria-hidden=\&quot;true\&quot; class=\&quot;relative sm:absolute sm:-left-5\&quot;&gt;\n        &lt;svg width=\&quot;16\&quot; height=\&quot;16\&quot; viewBox=\&quot;0 0 24 24\&quot;&gt;&lt;path fill=\&quot;currentcolor\&quot; d=\&quot;m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z\&quot;&gt;&lt;/path&gt;&lt;/svg&gt;\n      &lt;/a&gt;\n    &lt;/div&gt;\n    &lt;p&gt;With the draft standards available today, we do not have a lot of options.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;If we simply switch to ML-DSA-44 for all signatures, weâre adding 15kB of data that needs to be transmitted from the server to the client during the TLS handshake. Is that a lot? Probably. We will address that later on.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;If we wait a bit and replace all but the handshake signature with FN-DSA-512, weâre looking at adding only 7kB. Thatâs much better, but I have to repeat that itâs difficult to implement FN-DSA-512 signing safely without timing side channels, and there is a good chance weâll shoot ourselves in the foot if weâre not careful. Another way to shoot ourselves in the foot &lt;i&gt;today&lt;/i&gt; is with stateful hash-based signatures, as we explain &lt;a href=\&quot;https://blog.cloudflare.com/pq-2024/#stateful-hash-based-signatures\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;here&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;. All in all, FN-DSA-512 and stateful hash-based signatures tempt us with a similar and clear performance benefit over ML-DSA-44, but are difficult to use safely.&lt;/p&gt;\n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h3 id=\&quot;signatures-on-the-horizon\&quot;&gt;Signatures on the horizon&lt;/h3&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#signatures-on-the-horizon\&quot; aria-hidden=\&quot;true\&quot; class=\&quot;relative sm:absolute sm:-left-5\&quot;&gt;\n        &lt;svg width=\&quot;16\&quot; height=\&quot;16\&quot; viewBox=\&quot;0 0 24 24\&quot;&gt;&lt;path fill=\&quot;currentcolor\&quot; d=\&quot;m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z\&quot;&gt;&lt;/path&gt;&lt;/svg&gt;\n      &lt;/a&gt;\n    &lt;/div&gt;\n    &lt;p&gt;There are some promising new signature schemes submitted to the NIST onramp.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;Purely looking at sizes, SQISign I is the clear winner, even beating RSA-2048. Unfortunately, the computation required for signing, and crucially verification, are too high. SQISign is in a worse position than FN-DSA with implementation security: itâs very complicated and itâs unclear how to perform signing in &lt;i&gt;constant time&lt;/i&gt;. For niche applications, SQISign might be useful, but for general adoption verification times need to improve significantly, even if that requires a larger signature. Over the last few years there has been amazing progress in improving verification time; simplifying the algorithm; and &lt;a href=\&quot;https://eprint.iacr.org/2025/832\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;implementation security&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; for (variants of) SQISign. Theyâre not there yet, but the gap has shrunk much more than weâd have expected. If the pace of improvement holds, then a future SQISign could well be viable for TLS.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;One conservative contender is &lt;a href=\&quot;https://link.springer.com/chapter/10.1007/3-540-48910-X_15\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;UOV (unbalanced oil and vinegar)&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;. It is an old multivariate scheme with a large public key (66.5kB), but small signatures (96 bytes). Over the decades, there have been many attempts to add some structure to UOV public keys, to get a better balance between public key and signature size. Many of these so-called &lt;i&gt;structured multivariate &lt;/i&gt;schemes, which includes Rainbow and GeMMS, unfortunately have been broken dramatically &lt;a href=\&quot;https://eprint.iacr.org/2022/214.pdf\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;âwith a laptop over the weekendâ&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;. MAYO and SNOVA, which weâll get to in a bit, are the latest attempts at structured multivariate. UOV itself has remained mostly unscathed. Surprisingly in 2025, Lars Ran found a completely new &lt;a href=\&quot;https://eprint.iacr.org/2025/1143\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;âwedgesâ attack&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; on UOV. It doesnât affect UOV much, although SNOVA and MAYO are hit harder. Why the attack is noteworthy, is that itâs based on a relatively simple idea: it is surprising it wasnât found before. Now, getting back to performance: if we combine UOV for the root and SCTs with ML-DSA-44 for the others, weâre looking at only 10kB â close to FN-DSA-512.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;Now, letâs turn to the main event:&lt;/p&gt;\n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h3 id=\&quot;the-fight-between-mayo-versus-snova\&quot;&gt;The fight between MAYO versus SNOVA&lt;/h3&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#the-fight-between-mayo-versus-snova\&quot; aria-hidden=\&quot;true\&quot; class=\&quot;relative sm:absolute sm:-left-5\&quot;&gt;\n        &lt;svg width=\&quot;16\&quot; height=\&quot;16\&quot; viewBox=\&quot;0 0 24 24\&quot;&gt;&lt;path fill=\&quot;currentcolor\&quot; d=\&quot;m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z\&quot;&gt;&lt;/path&gt;&lt;/svg&gt;\n      &lt;/a&gt;\n    &lt;/div&gt;\n    &lt;p&gt;Looking at the roster today, MAYO and particularly SNOVA look great from a performance standpoint. Last year, SNOVA and MAYO were closer in performance, but they have diverged quite a bit.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;&lt;a href=\&quot;https://pqmayo.org/\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;MAYO&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; is designed by the cryptographer that broke &lt;a href=\&quot;https://eprint.iacr.org/2022/214.pdf\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;Rainbow&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;. As a structured multivariate scheme, its security requires careful scrutiny, but its utility (assuming it is not broken) is very appealing. MAYO allows for a fine-grained tradeoff between signature and public key size. For the submission, to keep things simple, the authors proposed two concrete variants: MAYO&lt;sub&gt;one&lt;/sub&gt; with balanced signature (454 bytes) and public key (1.4kB) sizes, and MAYO&lt;sub&gt;two&lt;/sub&gt; that has signatures of 216 bytes, while keeping the public key manageable at 4.3kB. Verification times are excellent, while signing times are somewhat slower than ECDSA, but far better than RSA. Combining both variants in the obvious way, weâre only looking at 4.3kB. These numbers are a bit higher than last year, as MAYO adjusted its parameters again slightly to account for newly discovered attacks.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;Over the competition, &lt;a href=\&quot;https://snova.pqclab.org/\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;SNOVA&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; has been hit harder by attacks than MAYO. SNOVAâs response has been more aggressive: instead of just tweaking parameters to adjust, they have also made larger changes to the internals of the scheme, to counter the attacks and to get a performance improvement to boot. Combining SNOVA&lt;sub&gt;(37,17,16,2)&lt;/sub&gt; and SNOVA&lt;sub&gt;(24,5,23,4)&lt;/sub&gt; in the obvious way, weâre looking at adding just an amazing 2.1kB.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;We see a face-off shaping up between the risky but much smaller SNOVA, and the conservative but slower MAYO. Zooming out, both have very welcome performance, and both are too risky to deploy now. Ranâs new wedges attack is an example that the field of multivariate cryptanalysis still holds surprises, and needs more eyes and time. Itâs too soon to pick a winner between SNOVA and MAYO: let them continue to compete. Even if they turn out to be secure, neither is likely to be standardized by 2029, which means we cannot rely on them for the initial migration to post-quantum authentication.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;Stepping back, is the 15kB for ML-DSA-44 actually that bad?&lt;/p&gt;\n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h2 id=\&quot;do-we-really-care-about-the-extra-bytes\&quot;&gt;Do we really care about the extra bytes?&lt;/h2&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#do-we-really-care-about-the-extra-bytes\&quot; aria-hidden=\&quot;true\&quot; class=\&quot;relative sm:absolute sm:-left-5\&quot;&gt;\n        &lt;svg width=\&quot;16\&quot; height=\&quot;16\&quot; viewBox=\&quot;0 0 24 24\&quot;&gt;&lt;path fill=\&quot;currentcolor\&quot; d=\&quot;m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z\&quot;&gt;&lt;/path&gt;&lt;/svg&gt;\n      &lt;/a&gt;\n    &lt;/div&gt;\n    &lt;p&gt;On average, around 18 million TLS connections are established with Cloudflare per second. Upgrading each to ML-DSA, would take 2.1Tbps, which is 0.5% of our current total network capacity. No problem so far. The question is how these extra bytes affect performance.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;It will take 15kB extra to swap in ML-DSA-44. Thatâs a lot compared to the typical handshake today, but itâs not a lot compared to the JavaScript and images served on many web pages. The key point is that the change we must make here affects every single TLS connection, whether itâs used for a bloated website, or a time-critical API call. Also, itâs not just about waiting a bit longer. If you have spotty cellular reception, that extra data can make the difference between being able to load a page, and having the connection time out. (As an aside, talking about bloat: many apps perform a &lt;a href=\&quot;https://thomwiggers.nl/publication/tls-on-android/tls-on-android.pdf\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;surprisingly high number of TLS handshakes&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;Just like with key agreement, performance isnât our only concern: we also want the connection to succeed in the first place. Back in 2021, &lt;a href=\&quot;https://blog.cloudflare.com/sizing-up-post-quantum-signatures/\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;we ran an experiment&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; artificially enlarging the certificate chain to simulate larger post-quantum certificates. We summarize the result &lt;a href=\&quot;https://blog.cloudflare.com/pq-2024/#do-we-really-care-about-the-extra-bytes\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;here&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;. One key take-away is that some clients or middleboxes donât like certificate chains larger than 10kB. This is problematic for a &lt;a href=\&quot;https://eprint.iacr.org/2018/063.pdf\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;single-certificate migration&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; strategy. In this approach, the server installs a single traditional certificate that contains a separate post-quantum certificate in a so-called non-critical extension. A client that does not support post-quantum certificates will ignore the extension. In this approach, installing the single certificate will immediately break all clients with compatibility issues, making it a non-starter. On the performance side there is also a steep drop in performance at 10kB because of the initial congestion window.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;\n&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;Is 9kB too much? The slowdown in TLS handshake time would be approximately 15%. We felt the latter is workable, but far from ideal: such a slowdown is noticeable and people might hold off deploying post-quantum certificates before itâs too late.\n&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;Chrome is more cautious and set 10% as their target for maximum TLS handshake time regression. They &lt;a href=\&quot;https://dadrian.io/blog/posts/pqc-signatures-2024/#fnref:3\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;report&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; that deploying post-quantum key agreement has already incurred a 4% slowdown in TLS handshake time, for the extra 1.1kB from server-to-client and 1.2kB from client-to-server. That slowdown is proportionally larger than the 15% we found for 9kB, but that could be explained by slower upload speeds than download speeds.Â &lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;There has been pushback against the focus on TLS handshake times. One argument is that session resumption alleviates the need for sending the certificates again. A second argument is that the data required to visit a typical website dwarfs the additional bytes for post-quantum certificates. One example is this &lt;a href=\&quot;https://www.amazon.science/publications/the-impact-of-data-heavy-post-quantum-tls-1-3-on-the-time-to-last-byte-of-real-world-connections\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;2024 publication&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;, where Amazon researchers have simulated the impact of large post-quantum certificates on data-heavy TLS connections. They argue that typical connections transfer multiple requests and hundreds of kilobytes, and for those the TLS handshake slowdown disappears in the margin.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;Are session resumption and hundreds of kilobytes over a connection typical though? Weâd like to share what we see. We focus on QUIC connections, which are likely initiated by browsers or browser-like clients. Of all QUIC connections with Cloudflare that carry at least one HTTP request, 27% are &lt;a href=\&quot;https://blog.cloudflare.com/even-faster-connection-establishment-with-quic-0-rtt-resumption/\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;resumptions&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;, meaning that key material from a previous TLS connection is reused, avoiding the need to transmit certificates. The median number of bytes transferred from server-to-client over a resumed QUIC connection is 4.4kB, while the average is 259kB. For non-resumptions the median is 8.1kB and average is 583kB. This vast difference between median and average indicates that a small fraction of data-heavy connections skew the average. In fact, only 15.5% of all QUIC connections transfer more than 100kB.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;The median certificate chain today (with compression) is &lt;a href=\&quot;https://datatracker.ietf.org/doc/html/draft-ietf-tls-cert-abridge-02#section-4\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;3.2kB&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;. That means that almost 40% of all data transferred from server to client on more than half of the non-resumed QUIC connections are just for the certificates, and this only gets worse with post-quantum algorithms. For the majority of QUIC connections, using ML-DSA-44 as a drop-in replacement for classical signatures would more than double the number of transmitted bytes over the lifetime of the connection.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;It sounds quite bad if the vast majority of data transferred for a typical connection is just for the post-quantum certificates. Itâs still only a proxy for what is actually important: the effect on metrics relevant to the end-user, such as the browsing experience (e.g. &lt;a href=\&quot;https://web.dev/articles/optimize-lcp\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;largest contentful paint&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;) and the amount of data those certificates take from a userâs monthly data cap. We will continue to investigate and get a better understanding of the impact.&lt;/p&gt;\n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h2 id=\&quot;way-forward-for-post-quantum-authentication\&quot;&gt;Way forward for post-quantum authentication&lt;/h2&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#way-forward-for-post-quantum-authentication\&quot; aria-hidden=\&quot;true\&quot; class=\&quot;relative sm:absolute sm:-left-5\&quot;&gt;\n        &lt;svg width=\&quot;16\&quot; height=\&quot;16\&quot; viewBox=\&quot;0 0 24 24\&quot;&gt;&lt;path fill=\&quot;currentcolor\&quot; d=\&quot;m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z\&quot;&gt;&lt;/path&gt;&lt;/svg&gt;\n      &lt;/a&gt;\n    &lt;/div&gt;\n    &lt;p&gt;The path for migrating the Internet to post-quantum authentication is much less clear than with key agreement. Unless we can get performance much closer to todayâs authentication, we expect the vast majority to keep post-quantum authentication disabled. Postponing enabling post-quantum authentication until Q-day draws near carries a real risk that we will not see the issues before itâs too late to fix. Thatâs why itâs essential to make post-quantum authentication performant enough to be turned on by default.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;Weâre exploring various ideas to reduce the number of signatures, in increasing order of ambition: leaving out intermediates; KEMTLS; and Merkle Tree Certificates. We covered these in &lt;a href=\&quot;https://blog.cloudflare.com/pq-2024/#reducing-number-of-signatures\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;detail last year&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;. Most progress has been made on the last one: &lt;a href=\&quot;https://datatracker.ietf.org/doc/draft-davidben-tls-merkle-tree-certs/\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;Merkle Tree Certificates&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; (MTC). In this proposal, in the common case, all signatures except the handshake signature are replaced by a short &amp;lt;800 byte Merkle tree proof. This could well allow for post-quantum authentication thatâs actually faster than using traditional certificates today! Together with Chrome, weâre going to try it out by the end of the year: read about it in &lt;a href=\&quot;https://blog.cloudflare.com/bootstrap-mtc/\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;this blog&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; post.&lt;/p&gt;\n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h3 id=\&quot;not-just-tls-authentication-and-key-agreement\&quot;&gt;Not just TLS, authentication, and key agreement&lt;/h3&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#not-just-tls-authentication-and-key-agreement\&quot; aria-hidden=\&quot;true\&quot; class=\&quot;relative sm:absolute sm:-left-5\&quot;&gt;\n        &lt;svg width=\&quot;16\&quot; height=\&quot;16\&quot; viewBox=\&quot;0 0 24 24\&quot;&gt;&lt;path fill=\&quot;currentcolor\&quot; d=\&quot;m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z\&quot;&gt;&lt;/path&gt;&lt;/svg&gt;\n      &lt;/a&gt;\n    &lt;/div&gt;\n    &lt;p&gt;Despite its length, in this blog post, we have only really touched upon migrating TLS. And even TLS we did not cover completely, as we have not discussed &lt;a href=\&quot;https://blog.cloudflare.com/announcing-encrypted-client-hello\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;Encrypted ClientHello&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; (we didnât forget about it). Although important, TLS is not the only protocol key to the security of the Internet. We want to briefly mention a few other challenges, but cannot go into detail. One particular challenge is DNSSEC, which is responsible for securing the resolution of domain names.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;Although key agreement and signatures are the most widely used cryptographic primitives, over the last few years we have seen the adoption of more &lt;a href=\&quot;https://github.com/fancy-cryptography/fancy-cryptography\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;esoteric cryptography&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; to serve more advanced use cases, such as unlinkable tokens with &lt;a href=\&quot;https://blog.cloudflare.com/privacy-pass-standard\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;Privacy Pass&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; / &lt;a href=\&quot;https://blog.cloudflare.com/eliminating-captchas-on-iphones-and-macs-using-new-standardhttps://blog.cloudflare.com/eliminating-captchas-on-iphones-and-macs-using-new-standard\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;PAT&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;, anonymous credentials, and &lt;a href=\&quot;https://blog.cloudflare.com/inside-geo-key-manager-v2\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;attribute based encryption&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; to name a few. For most of these advanced cryptographic schemes, there is no known practical post-quantum alternative yet. Although to our delight there have been great advances in post-quantum anonymous credentials.&lt;/p&gt;\n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h2 id=\&quot;what-you-can-do-today-to-stay-safe-against-quantum-attacks\&quot;&gt;What you can do today to stay safe against quantum attacks&lt;/h2&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#what-you-can-do-today-to-stay-safe-against-quantum-attacks\&quot; aria-hidden=\&quot;true\&quot; class=\&quot;relative sm:absolute sm:-left-5\&quot;&gt;\n        &lt;svg width=\&quot;16\&quot; height=\&quot;16\&quot; viewBox=\&quot;0 0 24 24\&quot;&gt;&lt;path fill=\&quot;currentcolor\&quot; d=\&quot;m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z\&quot;&gt;&lt;/path&gt;&lt;/svg&gt;\n      &lt;/a&gt;\n    &lt;/div&gt;\n    &lt;p&gt;To summarize, there are two main post-quantum migrations to keep an eye on: key agreement, and certificates.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;We recommend moving to &lt;b&gt;post-quantum key agreement &lt;/b&gt;to counter store-now/decrypt-later attacks, which only requires a software update on both sides. That means that with the quick adoption (weâre &lt;a href=\&quot;https://developers.cloudflare.com/ssl/post-quantum-cryptography/pqc-support/\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;keeping a list&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;) of X25519MLKEM768 across software and services, you might well be secure already against store-now/decrypt-later! On Cloudflare Radar you can &lt;a href=\&quot;https://radar.cloudflare.com/adoption-and-usage#browser-support\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;check&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; whether your browser supports X25519MLKEM768; if you use Firefox, there is &lt;a href=\&quot;https://addons.mozilla.org/en-US/firefox/addon/pqspy/\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;an extension&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; to check support of websites while you visit; you can scan whether your website supports it &lt;a href=\&quot;https://pqscan.io/\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;here&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;; and you can use Wireshark to check for it &lt;a href=\&quot;https://www.netmeister.org/blog/tls-hybrid-kex.html\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;on the wire&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;Those are just spot checks. For a proper migration, youâll need to figure out where cryptography is used. Thatâs a tall order, as most organizations have a hard time tracking all software, services, and external vendors they use in the first place. There will be systems that are difficult to upgrade or have external dependencies, but in many cases itâs simple. In fact, in many cases, youâll spend a lot of time to find out that they are already done.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;As figuring out &lt;i&gt;what to do&lt;/i&gt; is the bulk of the work, itâs perhaps tempting to split that out as a first milestone: create a detailed inventory first; the so-called &lt;a href=\&quot;https://github.com/IBM/CBOM\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;cryptographic bill of materials&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; (CBOM). Donât let an inventory become a goal on its own: we need to keep our eyes on the ball. Most cases are easy: if you figured out what to do to migrate in one case, donât wait and context switch, but just do it. That doesnât mean itâll be fast: this is a marathon not a sprint, but youâll be surprised how much ground can be covered by getting started.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;&lt;b&gt;Certificates.&lt;/b&gt; At the time of writing this blog in October 2025, the final standards for post-quantum certificates are not set yet. Hopefully that wonât take too long to resolve. But there is much that you can do now to prepare for post-quantum certificates that you wonât regret at all. Keep software up-to-date. Automate certificate issuance. Ensure you can install multiple certificates.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;In case youâre worried about protocol ossification, there is no reason to wait: the final post-quantum standards will not be very different from the draft. You can test with preliminary implementations (or large dummy certificates) today.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;The post-quantum migration is quite unique. Typically, if cryptography is broken, itâs either sudden or gradually making it easy to ignore for a time. In both cases, migrations in the end are rushed. With the quantum threat, we know for sure that weâll need to replace a lot of cryptography, but we also have time. Instead of just a chore, we invite you to see this as an opportunity: we have to do maintenance now on many systems that rarely get touched. Instead of just hotfixes, now is the opportunity to rethink past choices. &lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;At least, if you start now. Good luck with your migration, and if you hit any issues, do reach out: ask-research@cloudflare.com&lt;/p&gt;&quot;],&quot;published_at&quot;:[0,&quot;2025-10-28T13:00+00:00&quot;],&quot;updated_at&quot;:[0,&quot;2025-10-28T19:28:20.942Z&quot;],&quot;feature_image&quot;:[0,&quot;https://cf-assets.www.cloudflare.com/zkvhlag99gkb/43eV4y4luzdIkCKwgy2z6/62d96dd3dbac8c200da8eb3d8000e26e/image4.png&quot;],&quot;tags&quot;:[1,[[0,{&quot;id&quot;:[0,&quot;1x7tpPmKIUCt19EDgM1Tsl&quot;],&quot;name&quot;:[0,&quot;Research&quot;],&quot;slug&quot;:[0,&quot;research&quot;]}],[0,{&quot;id&quot;:[0,&quot;6bIo7ayy56Fzdrtf9z2EWy&quot;],&quot;name&quot;:[0,&quot;Post-Quantum&quot;],&quot;slug&quot;:[0,&quot;post-quantum&quot;]}]]],&quot;relatedTags&quot;:[0],&quot;authors&quot;:[1,[[0,{&quot;name&quot;:[0,&quot;Bas Westerbaan&quot;],&quot;slug&quot;:[0,&quot;bas&quot;],&quot;bio&quot;:[0,null],&quot;profile_image&quot;:[0,&quot;https://cf-assets.www.cloudflare.com/zkvhlag99gkb/4KeBG8XO1ADZHuEV9v5Hwz/6c90a96cfb1373b208bffc35f3fd71f4/bas.png&quot;],&quot;location&quot;:[0,&quot;The Netherlands&quot;],&quot;website&quot;:[0,&quot;https://bas.westerbaan.name&quot;],&quot;twitter&quot;:[0,&quot;@bwesterb&quot;],&quot;facebook&quot;:[0,null],&quot;publiclyIndex&quot;:[0,true]}]]],&quot;meta_description&quot;:[0,&quot;Today over half of human-initiated traffic with Cloudflare is protected against harvest-now/decrypt-later with post-quantum encryption.
