Export traces to any OpenTelemetry-compatible provider for deeper application observability -- no code changes required&quot;],&quot;imgPreview&quot;:[0,&quot;https://cf-assets.www.cloudflare.com/zkvhlag99gkb/5c4yTg0GM9UzBdAOSLEgaR/b356b457c8cc750fa7a2c774fa953074/Announcing_Workers_automatic_tracing_--__now_in_open_beta.png&quot;]}],&quot;publicly_index&quot;:[0,true]}],[0,{&quot;id&quot;:[0,&quot;20pf9BGcV10k0j9ASL8JtY&quot;],&quot;title&quot;:[0,&quot;The tricky science of Internet measurement&quot;],&quot;slug&quot;:[0,&quot;tricky-internet-measurement&quot;],&quot;excerpt&quot;:[0,&quot;The Internet is one big open system composed of many closed boxes â which makes measuring the Internet difficult. In this post we explore Internet measurement as a science.&quot;],&quot;featured&quot;:[0,false],&quot;html&quot;:[0,&quot;&lt;p&gt;Measurement is critical to our understanding not just of the world and the universe, but also the systems we design and deploy. The Internet is no exception but the challenges of measuring the Internet are unique.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;The Internet is remarkably opaque, which is counter-intuitive given its open and multi-stakeholder model. Itâs opaque because ultimately the Internet joins many networks and services that are each owned and operated by unrelated entities, and that rarely share or report about their systems. Every network may carry and forward what other systems produce, but each system is entirely independent â which, to be honest, is the magic of the Internet. Itâs in this opaque-yet-critical context that Internet measurement must exist as a scientific practice, with all the associated rigor, repeatability, and reproduction.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;Measurement as a scientific practice can be exciting â for what it gets right as well as wrong. The following statement encapsulates some of the subtleties:&lt;/p&gt;&lt;blockquote&gt;&lt;p&gt;â&lt;b&gt;5 out of 6 scientists say that &lt;/b&gt;&lt;a href=\&quot;https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_roulette\&quot;&gt;&lt;b&gt;&lt;u&gt;Russian Roulette&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/b&gt;&lt;/a&gt;&lt;b&gt; is safe.â&lt;/b&gt;&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/blockquote&gt;&lt;p&gt;The statement is absurd! Laugh as we might, the statement is also logical. Itâs trivially easy to design an experiment that leads to the above statement. However, the only way this experiment could succeed is if the âactorâ â that is, whoever conducts the experiment â ignores every aspect of measurement science that makes the practice credible, as follows.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;ul&gt;&lt;li&gt;&lt;p&gt;&lt;b&gt;Methodology&lt;/b&gt;: a cycle consisting of data curation, modeling, and validation. Here, the experiment (data curation) could only succeed if each participant is prevented from seeing othersâ injuries. More importantly, no measurement is needed because the actor can calculate probabilities with available numbers, without the experiment!&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/li&gt;&lt;li&gt;&lt;p&gt;&lt;b&gt;Ethics&lt;/b&gt;: the way we measure can have undue, undesirable consequences. A bare minimum principle is &lt;i&gt;do no harm.&lt;/i&gt;&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/li&gt;&lt;li&gt;&lt;p&gt;&lt;b&gt;Representation&lt;/b&gt;: clear and complete statements or visualizations should be at least informative and ideally actionable; otherwise, they can be misleading. Say each participant answered with yes to the question, âare you safe?â They are answering a different question than âis the game safe?â&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/li&gt;&lt;/ul&gt;&lt;p&gt;In this blog we look at each of the above aspects of measurement, describe how they manifest in the Internet space, and relate them to examples from work that will be featured throughout &lt;a href=\&quot;https://blog.cloudflare.com/internet-measurement-resilience-transparency-week\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;the week&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;. Letâs first start with some background.&lt;/p&gt;\n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h2 id=\&quot;preface-a-motivating-example-from-inside-cloudflare\&quot;&gt;Preface: A motivating example from inside Cloudflare&lt;/h2&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#preface-a-motivating-example-from-inside-cloudflare\&quot; aria-hidden=\&quot;true\&quot; class=\&quot;relative sm:absolute sm:-left-5\&quot;&gt;\n        &lt;svg width=\&quot;16\&quot; height=\&quot;16\&quot; viewBox=\&quot;0 0 24 24\&quot;&gt;&lt;path fill=\&quot;currentcolor\&quot; d=\&quot;m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z\&quot;&gt;&lt;/path&gt;&lt;/svg&gt;\n      &lt;/a&gt;\n    &lt;/div&gt;\n    &lt;p&gt;High quality measurements help to identify, understand, even explain our experiences, environments, and systems. However, observation in isolation, without context, can be perilous. The following is a time series from an internal graph of HTTP requests from Lviv, Ukraine, leading up to the evening of 28 February 2022:&lt;/p&gt;\n          &lt;figure class=\&quot;kg-card kg-image-card\&quot;&gt;\n          &lt;Image src=\&quot;https://cf-assets.www.cloudflare.com/zkvhlag99gkb/7D1hr8mMykICnj7Rh1Apyf/9b50dd98d996ed296fbad64cdbada497/image9.png\&quot; alt=\&quot;\&quot; class=\&quot;kg-image\&quot; width=\&quot;1805\&quot; height=\&quot;747\&quot; loading=\&quot;lazy\&quot;/&gt;\n          &lt;/figure&gt;&lt;p&gt;On that day, traffic from the region increased by 3-4X. For context, the Russian incursion into Ukraine began four days earlier. The world was watching events closely. Cloudflare was no exception, helping both to &lt;a href=\&quot;https://blog.cloudflare.com/internet-traffic-patterns-in-ukraine-since-february-21-2022/\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;report&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; and to &lt;a href=\&quot;https://blog.cloudflare.com/steps-taken-around-cloudflares-services-in-ukraine-belarus-and-russia/\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;mitigate&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; network effects.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;Upon observing that abnormal spike, we at Cloudflare &lt;i&gt;could have&lt;/i&gt; mistakenly reported the increase as a potential DoS attack. However, there were counter-indications. First, no attack was flagged by the DoS defense and mitigation systems. In addition, the profile was atypical of attack traffic, which tends to be either single source from a single location or multiple sources from multiple locations. In this instance the increase came from multiple source networks but in a single location (Lviv).&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;Cloudflare had the tools to avoid erroneous reporting and later &lt;a href=\&quot;https://blog.cloudflare.com/internet-traffic-patterns-in-ukraine-since-february-21-2022/#internet-traffic\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;correctly reported&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; that the increase was due to a mass of people converging in Lviv, the city with the last train station on the westward journey out of Ukraine. But â and this is important in a measurement context â nothing visible from Cloudflareâs perspective could provide an explanation. In the end, an employee saw a report on BBC about the massive movement of people in that part of Ukraine, which enabled us to better explain the traffic shift.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;This example is an important reminder to always look for alternative explanations. It also shows how observations alone can lead to wrong conclusions, due to missing information or unrecognized biases. But good numbers without bias &lt;a href=\&quot;https://blog.cloudflare.com/loving-performance-measurements/\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;can be misunderstood&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;, too.&lt;/p&gt;\n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h2 id=\&quot;measurement-vocabulary-and-jargon\&quot;&gt;Measurement vocabulary and jargon&lt;/h2&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#measurement-vocabulary-and-jargon\&quot; aria-hidden=\&quot;true\&quot; class=\&quot;relative sm:absolute sm:-left-5\&quot;&gt;\n        &lt;svg width=\&quot;16\&quot; height=\&quot;16\&quot; viewBox=\&quot;0 0 24 24\&quot;&gt;&lt;path fill=\&quot;currentcolor\&quot; d=\&quot;m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z\&quot;&gt;&lt;/path&gt;&lt;/svg&gt;\n      &lt;/a&gt;\n    &lt;/div&gt;\n    &lt;p&gt;In the measurement context there is a vocabulary of common words with specific meanings that are useful to know before diving into practice and examples.&lt;/p&gt;\n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h3 id=\&quot;active-and-passive-measurement\&quot;&gt;Active and passive measurementÂ &lt;/h3&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#active-and-passive-measurement\&quot; aria-hidden=\&quot;true\&quot; class=\&quot;relative sm:absolute sm:-left-5\&quot;&gt;\n        &lt;svg width=\&quot;16\&quot; height=\&quot;16\&quot; viewBox=\&quot;0 0 24 24\&quot;&gt;&lt;path fill=\&quot;currentcolor\&quot; d=\&quot;m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z\&quot;&gt;&lt;/path&gt;&lt;/svg&gt;\n      &lt;/a&gt;\n    &lt;/div&gt;\n    &lt;p&gt;These describe the âhow.â In an &lt;i&gt;active&lt;/i&gt; measurement, an actor initiates some action designed to trigger a response. The response may be data, such as latency returned from a ping or a DNS answer in response to a query. The response may be an observable change in a mechanism or system triggered by an action, such as well-crafted probe packets that prompt reactions from and expose middleboxes.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;In a &lt;i&gt;passive&lt;/i&gt; measurement, the actor only observes. No action is taken. As a result, no response is triggered; the system and its behaviour are unaltered. Logs are typically compiled from passive observations, and Cloudflareâs own are no exception. The vast majority of data shown in &lt;a href=\&quot;https://radar.cloudflare.com\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;Cloudflare Radar&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; derives from those logs.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;Each has its trade-offs. Active measurements are targeted and can be controlled. They are also exceptionally difficult (and often costly) to scale and, as a result, are only able to observe the parts of a system where they are deployed. Conversely, passive measurements tend to be lighter weight, but only succeed if the observer is at the right place at the right time.Â &lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;Effectively, the two methods complement each other, and that makes them most powerful when orchestrated so that the knowledge from one feeds into the other. For example, in our own prior attempts to &lt;a href=\&quot;https://blog.cloudflare.com/cdn-latency-passive-measurement/\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;understand performance across CDNs&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;, we interrogated the (passive) request logs to get insights, which helped inform later (active) pings using RIPEâs Atlas that we used to confirm our insights and results. In the opposite direction, our efforts to (passively) &lt;a href=\&quot;https://blog.cloudflare.com/connection-tampering/\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;detect and understand connection failures&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; was informed by, and arguably only possible because of, a large body of (active) measurements in the research community to understand wide-scale connection tampering.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;For more on the interplay between active and passive, you can read about the experience of a researcher who was equipped to &lt;a href=\&quot;https://blog.cloudflare.com/experience-of-data-at-scale\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;dig deep&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; into Cloudflareâs vast troves of data because of insights from prior active measurements in the research community.&lt;/p&gt;\n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h3 id=\&quot;direct-and-indirect-measurement\&quot;&gt;Direct and indirect measurementÂ &lt;/h3&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#direct-and-indirect-measurement\&quot; aria-hidden=\&quot;true\&quot; class=\&quot;relative sm:absolute sm:-left-5\&quot;&gt;\n        &lt;svg width=\&quot;16\&quot; height=\&quot;16\&quot; viewBox=\&quot;0 0 24 24\&quot;&gt;&lt;path fill=\&quot;currentcolor\&quot; d=\&quot;m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z\&quot;&gt;&lt;/path&gt;&lt;/svg&gt;\n      &lt;/a&gt;\n    &lt;/div&gt;\n    &lt;p&gt;It is possible to gain insights about something without directly observing it. Consider, for example, the capacity of a path, better known as the &lt;i&gt;bandwidth&lt;/i&gt;. The common method to &lt;i&gt;directly&lt;/i&gt; observe bandwidth is to launch a &lt;a href=\&quot;https://speed.cloudflare.com/\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;speed test&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;. Itâs a simple test, but it has two problems.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;The first is that it works by consuming as much of the bandwidth as it can (which creates an ethical dilemma we later revisit). The second is that it actually measures throughput from a sender to a receiver, which is the available bandwidth (or, alternately, the residual capacity) of the &lt;i&gt;bottleneck&lt;/i&gt; link. If two speed tests share a bottleneck then each might observe throughput that is Â½ of the actual bandwidth. The evidence is in the numbers, as seen below, where observations of a speed test range from 69-85Mbps â thatâs a +/- range of nearly 20% from the median, and far from a fixed value!&lt;/p&gt;\n          &lt;figure class=\&quot;kg-card kg-image-card\&quot;&gt;\n          &lt;Image src=\&quot;https://cf-assets.www.cloudflare.com/zkvhlag99gkb/6OpXXM8CqkhWkbavw9RgMv/395827e2390fa145650703905c4abdb4/image2.png\&quot; alt=\&quot;\&quot; class=\&quot;kg-image\&quot; width=\&quot;1194\&quot; height=\&quot;608\&quot; loading=\&quot;lazy\&quot;/&gt;\n          &lt;/figure&gt;&lt;p&gt;There is instead a 25+ year-old &lt;i&gt;indirect&lt;/i&gt; alternative to speed tests called &lt;a href=\&quot;https://www.usenix.org/legacy/publications/library/proceedings/usits01/full_papers/lai/lai_html/node2.html\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;the packet pair&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;, or packet train. It works by first transmitting pairs of packets with no delay between them and recording their transmission times, then recording their arrival times. The change between transmission and arrival times of the two packets gives an indication of the bottleneck bandwidth. Repeat the packet pair probes and, with some statistical analysis, a good estimate of the true bottleneck bandwidth emerges. Instead of directly observing bandwidth by pushing and counting bytes over time, the packet pair technique uses the time between two packets to &lt;i&gt;indirectly&lt;/i&gt; calculate â or infer â the metric.&lt;/p&gt;\n          &lt;figure class=\&quot;kg-card kg-image-card\&quot;&gt;\n          &lt;Image src=\&quot;https://cf-assets.www.cloudflare.com/zkvhlag99gkb/4LMXzWWY1rbU0Tb02v7uzt/6e83b407e8cece51c3fa42b91bd036b3/image5.png\&quot; alt=\&quot;\&quot; class=\&quot;kg-image\&quot; width=\&quot;1999\&quot; height=\&quot;1362\&quot; loading=\&quot;lazy\&quot;/&gt;\n          &lt;/figure&gt;\n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h2 id=\&quot;the-network-measurement-lifecycle\&quot;&gt;The (Network) Measurement Lifecycle&lt;/h2&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#the-network-measurement-lifecycle\&quot; aria-hidden=\&quot;true\&quot; class=\&quot;relative sm:absolute sm:-left-5\&quot;&gt;\n        &lt;svg width=\&quot;16\&quot; height=\&quot;16\&quot; viewBox=\&quot;0 0 24 24\&quot;&gt;&lt;path fill=\&quot;currentcolor\&quot; d=\&quot;m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z\&quot;&gt;&lt;/path&gt;&lt;/svg&gt;\n      &lt;/a&gt;\n    &lt;/div&gt;\n    &lt;p&gt;Measurements are most powerful when they lead to reasonable predictions. Sometimes the predictions confirm our understanding of the world and systems we deploy into it. Occasionally, the predictions reveal something new. Either way, predictive measurements emerge by following a simple pattern: curate data, construct a model based on the data, then validate the model with (ideally) different data. Together, these create a measurement lifecycle.&lt;/p&gt;\n          &lt;figure class=\&quot;kg-card kg-image-card\&quot;&gt;\n          &lt;Image src=\&quot;https://cf-assets.www.cloudflare.com/zkvhlag99gkb/2bnJ1aWYRag3edCAUfEnkj/87ee90c52223d03120e7bc2d7df5c72b/image8.png\&quot; alt=\&quot;\&quot; class=\&quot;kg-image\&quot; width=\&quot;1360\&quot; height=\&quot;622\&quot; loading=\&quot;lazy\&quot;/&gt;\n          &lt;/figure&gt;&lt;p&gt;Ideally a measurement exercise encompasses the lifecycle from beginning to end, but there can be extremely valuable contributions and advances within each in isolation. Individual high-quality datasets are so difficult to curate that each can be a valid contribution. Similarly, with modeling techniques, or tools for validation. Measurement spans expert domains, and benefits from diverse skill sets.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;Letâs look at each step in order, beginning with data curation.&lt;/p&gt;\n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h2 id=\&quot;data-curation\&quot;&gt;Data curation&lt;/h2&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#data-curation\&quot; aria-hidden=\&quot;true\&quot; class=\&quot;relative sm:absolute sm:-left-5\&quot;&gt;\n        &lt;svg width=\&quot;16\&quot; height=\&quot;16\&quot; viewBox=\&quot;0 0 24 24\&quot;&gt;&lt;path fill=\&quot;currentcolor\&quot; d=\&quot;m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z\&quot;&gt;&lt;/path&gt;&lt;/svg&gt;\n      &lt;/a&gt;\n    &lt;/div&gt;\n    &lt;p&gt;The most common and familiar measurement exercise â often synonymous with measurement â is data gathering and curation. Data on its own can be fascinating and useful; &lt;a href=\&quot;https://radar.cloudflare.com\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;Cloudflare Radar&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; is clear evidence of that! Simple counting in many contexts can help us relate to and place our environments in context.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;Data gathering and curation consumes more energy, time, and resources than modeling or validation. The explanation is implied by the cyclical measurement pattern: validation requires a preceding model, and models are constructed using data. No data, no model, no validation, no insight nor prediction nor learning. The quality of each step in the cycle &lt;i&gt;depends&lt;/i&gt; on the quality of the previous step â high-quality data is &lt;i&gt;the&lt;/i&gt; linchpin in measurement practices. The &lt;a href=\&quot;https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Large_Hadron_Collider\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;Large Hadron Collider&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; and the &lt;a href=\&quot;https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Webb_Space_Telescope\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;James Webb Telescope&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; are great examples of how much we can, and need, to do â they operate relentlessly in pursuit of high-quality data. Similar âalways-onâ tools in the Internet measurement community are much less glamorous, but no less important. &lt;a href=\&quot;https://www.caida.org/about/\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;CAIDA&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; and &lt;a href=\&quot;https://atlas.ripe.net/\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;RIPEâs Atlas&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; are just two examples of longstanding projects that gather telemetry and curate datasets.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;Make no mistake: High-quality data gathering and curation is &lt;i&gt;hard&lt;/i&gt;.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;Luckily, âhigh-qualityâ does not mean perfect; it does mean &lt;i&gt;representative&lt;/i&gt;. For example, if weâre counting distance or time, the accuracy must reflect the true value. Large populations can be reasonably studied using much smaller numbers of samples. For example, our global assessment of connection tampering revealed valuable insights with a sample of &lt;a href=\&quot;https://blog.cloudflare.com/tcp-resets-timeouts/\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;1 in 10,000&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; (or 0.0001%). The low sampling rate works at Cloudflare in part because of the immense diversity of Cloudflareâs customers, which attracts traffic for all kinds of content and purposes. Later this week, weâll share in a blog post how imperfect signals used to find a sample of around 180,000 carrier-grade NATs in Cloudflareâs request logs are âgood enoughâ to identify more than 12,000,000 others that cannot be directly observed.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;Another important, and arguably counterintuitive, misconception is that more data naturally reveals more detail and answers to more questions. As Ram Sundaran writes in a &lt;a href=\&quot;https://blog.cloudflare.com/experience-of-data-at-scale\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;guest post&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;, sometimes there is so much noise that finding answers in large datasets can seem like a small miracle.&lt;/p&gt;\n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h2 id=\&quot;modeling\&quot;&gt;Modeling&lt;/h2&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#modeling\&quot; aria-hidden=\&quot;true\&quot; class=\&quot;relative sm:absolute sm:-left-5\&quot;&gt;\n        &lt;svg width=\&quot;16\&quot; height=\&quot;16\&quot; viewBox=\&quot;0 0 24 24\&quot;&gt;&lt;path fill=\&quot;currentcolor\&quot; d=\&quot;m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z\&quot;&gt;&lt;/path&gt;&lt;/svg&gt;\n      &lt;/a&gt;\n    &lt;/div&gt;\n    &lt;p&gt;Models may be conceptual, and describe aspects of an environment or system. The most useful can be expressed as simple statements about our understanding or our assumptions. In effect, they encapsulate a hypothesis that can be tested. For example, we might believe or assume that an ISP or network &lt;a href=\&quot;https://blog.cloudflare.com/cdn-latency-passive-measurement/#example\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;will typically prefer&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; a direct no-cost peering path to a CDN over transit network paths that incur a cost, even when the direct path is longer. This forms a model that can be validated.&lt;/p&gt;\n          &lt;figure class=\&quot;kg-card kg-image-card\&quot;&gt;\n          &lt;Image src=\&quot;https://cf-assets.www.cloudflare.com/zkvhlag99gkb/2rnYBJIYzjpAoerH3Z2bDL/2e2bcf155e2b1c3f6abe46a72fe129f5/image3.png\&quot; alt=\&quot;\&quot; class=\&quot;kg-image\&quot; width=\&quot;1380\&quot; height=\&quot;490\&quot; loading=\&quot;lazy\&quot;/&gt;\n          &lt;/figure&gt;&lt;p&gt;Predictive models push beyond our boundaries of understanding to help identify, explain, or understand aspects of systems that are not obvious or directly observable, or are difficult to ascertain. Predictive models often use statistical techniques to, for example, identify underlying stochastic processes or to create machine learning classifiers. A more common use of the statistical tools is to characterize the curated data itself. Remarkably powerful models can be simple probability distributions with means, medians, variance, and confidence indicators.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;One aspect of the Internet that attracted a lot of attention was how networks on the Internet choose to connect to other networks. Understanding how the Internet forms and grows is crucial for simulation, but also helps to predict ways in which networks might fail. The equation below on the left comes from the &lt;a href=\&quot;https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barab%C3%A1si%E2%80%93Albert_model\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;BarabÃ¡siâAlbert (B-A) model&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;, an early model that assumes &lt;i&gt;preferential connectivity&lt;/i&gt; or, in more familiar terms, ârich get richer.â&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;In its simplest version, a new network in the BA model chooses to connect to an existing network with a probability that is proportional to the number of connections of the existing networks. Later models did away with âintelligentâ selection mechanisms. The equation below on the right is based on the &lt;a href=\&quot;https://dl.acm.org/doi/pdf/10.1145/956981.956986\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;sizes of networks&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;, a more general mechanism similar to the way celestial bodies form in the universe.&lt;/p&gt;\n          &lt;figure class=\&quot;kg-card kg-image-card\&quot;&gt;\n          &lt;Image src=\&quot;https://cf-assets.www.cloudflare.com/zkvhlag99gkb/5h8V0ABHULoh2vRaa2wQhn/baf190909036b7f4f15fa506754784c5/Screenshot_2025-10-27_at_10.51.04.png\&quot; alt=\&quot;\&quot; class=\&quot;kg-image\&quot; width=\&quot;1516\&quot; height=\&quot;156\&quot; loading=\&quot;lazy\&quot;/&gt;\n          &lt;/figure&gt;&lt;p&gt;Sometimes knowing which tool to use and when is a skill in itself. One such example is throwing ML and AI at problems that are tractable with mechanisms that are simpler and far more transparent. This &lt;a href=\&quot;https://blog.cloudflare.com/experience-of-data-at-scale\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;guest blog&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;, for example, explains that ML was ruled out to understand anomalous TCP behaviour because TCP is tightly specified, which suggested that a full enumeration of various packet sequences was possibleâand proved correct.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;An understanding of the domain is often critical to our ability to construct accurate models. Machine learning, for example, is a useful tool to help make sense of large unstructured data, but can be remarkably powerful with some domain expertise. Our work featured later this week on detection of multi-user IPs provides one such example. In particular, we sought to detect carrier-grade NAT devices (CGNATs). They are unique among large-scale multiuser IPs because, unlike VPNs and proxies, users neither choose to use CGNATs nor are aware of their existence.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;The ML models successfully identified multiuser IPs, but disambiguating CGNATs proved elusive until we applied domain knowledge. For example, CGNATs are typically deployed across a range of contiguous IPs (e.g. in a /24 block) and, as shown below, turns out to be a very important feature in the model.&lt;/p&gt;\n          &lt;figure class=\&quot;kg-card kg-image-card\&quot;&gt;\n          &lt;Image src=\&quot;https://cf-assets.www.cloudflare.com/zkvhlag99gkb/15WI2U2JnD12WOcaCD9wQN/7bdf0adb9ade2444f7b3837f75c7f109/unnamed__1_.png\&quot; alt=\&quot;\&quot; class=\&quot;kg-image\&quot; width=\&quot;775\&quot; height=\&quot;374\&quot; loading=\&quot;lazy\&quot;/&gt;\n          &lt;/figure&gt;\n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h2 id=\&quot;validation\&quot;&gt;Validation&lt;/h2&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#validation\&quot; aria-hidden=\&quot;true\&quot; class=\&quot;relative sm:absolute sm:-left-5\&quot;&gt;\n        &lt;svg width=\&quot;16\&quot; height=\&quot;16\&quot; viewBox=\&quot;0 0 24 24\&quot;&gt;&lt;path fill=\&quot;currentcolor\&quot; d=\&quot;m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z\&quot;&gt;&lt;/path&gt;&lt;/svg&gt;\n      &lt;/a&gt;\n    &lt;/div&gt;\n    &lt;p&gt;The validation phase almost singularly determines the value of the whole measurement exercise, by testing the output of the model against data. If the model makes predictions that are reflected in the data, then the model has validity. Predictions that contrast or conflict with the validation data indicate that either the model is flawed or is biased by the curated data.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;Validation is where great measurement can fall apart â primarily in one of two ways. First, just like in the initial data curation phase, validation data must be representative of the population. For example, it would be a mistake to curate data about traffic during the day, build a model about that data, and then validate using data about traffic at night. There is also no point in using QUIC data to validate measurements about, say, TCP (unless the measurementâs hypothesis is that they have attributes in common). Care must always be taken to ensure that measurement cannot be corrupted by the differences between validation and initial data.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;Validation also risks being misleading when using the curated data, directly. Certainly this approach mitigates differences between datasets. However, the only conclusion that can be drawn when validating with the same data, is that the model reasonably describes the data ânot whatever the data represents. Consider, for example, machine learning. At its core, machine learning is a measurement in so much as it follows the lifecycle: curate data, (feed it into a machine learning algorithm to) build a model, then validate the output against data. An early common practice in the machine learning community was to partition a single dataset into 70% for training and 30% for validation. This is a setup that leads to a higher likelihood of a positive evaluation of the model that is not warranted, and potentially misleading. The best case for an ML model trained on a dataset that amplifies or omits important characteristics is a model that reflects those biases â which becomes a potential source of &lt;a href=\&quot;https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Algorithmic_bias\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;algorithmic bias&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;.Â &lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;Naturally we have greater confidence in models that prove valid with unrelated data. The validation dataset can describe the same attributes from a different source, for example, models constructed &lt;a href=\&quot;https://blog.cloudflare.com/cdn-latency-passive-measurement/\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;from passive RTT log data and validated against active pings&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;. Alternatively, models may be validated using entirely different data or signals, such as confirming &lt;a href=\&quot;https://blog.cloudflare.com/connection-tampering/\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;connection tampering with distributions and header values&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; that were ignored in the modelâs construction.Â &lt;/p&gt;\n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h2 id=\&quot;the-ethics-of-network-measurement\&quot;&gt;The ethics of network measurement&lt;/h2&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#the-ethics-of-network-measurement\&quot; aria-hidden=\&quot;true\&quot; class=\&quot;relative sm:absolute sm:-left-5\&quot;&gt;\n        &lt;svg width=\&quot;16\&quot; height=\&quot;16\&quot; viewBox=\&quot;0 0 24 24\&quot;&gt;&lt;path fill=\&quot;currentcolor\&quot; d=\&quot;m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z\&quot;&gt;&lt;/path&gt;&lt;/svg&gt;\n      &lt;/a&gt;\n    &lt;/div&gt;\n    &lt;p&gt;The importance of ethics in network measurement is hard to overstate. Itâs easy to perceive network measurement as risk-free, removed from and having little effect on humansâa perception far from truth. Recall the speed tests and the packet pair technique for bandwidth estimation described above. In a speed test, an actor estimates bandwidth by consuming all the available bottleneck capacity that may or may not be within the actorâs network. The cost of resource consumption might be borne by others, and certainly reduces the potential performance of the network for its users. The risks of that type of bandwidth measurement prompted the packet pair technique and its use of only a few pairs of packets and a little math to infer bandwidthâalbeit with some orchestration between a sender and receiver.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;Best practice in network measurement scrutinizes risks and effects &lt;i&gt;before&lt;/i&gt; the measurement exercise. This might seem like a burden, but the ethical considerations often spark creativity and are the reasons that novel methodology emerge. Looking for alternatives to JavaScript injection is what prompted Cloudflareâs own efforts to &lt;a href=\&quot;https://blog.cloudflare.com/cdn-latency-passive-measurement/\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;estimate the performance&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; of other CDNs using passive data. For more information, see â&lt;a href=\&quot;https://dl.acm.org/doi/10.1145/2896816\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;Ethical Considerations in Network Measurement Papers&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;â published in the Communications of the ACM (2016).&lt;/p&gt;\n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h2 id=\&quot;visualization-and-representation\&quot;&gt;Visualization and representation&lt;/h2&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#visualization-and-representation\&quot; aria-hidden=\&quot;true\&quot; class=\&quot;relative sm:absolute sm:-left-5\&quot;&gt;\n        &lt;svg width=\&quot;16\&quot; height=\&quot;16\&quot; viewBox=\&quot;0 0 24 24\&quot;&gt;&lt;path fill=\&quot;currentcolor\&quot; d=\&quot;m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z\&quot;&gt;&lt;/path&gt;&lt;/svg&gt;\n      &lt;/a&gt;\n    &lt;/div&gt;\n    &lt;p&gt;Visualization and representation are invaluable &lt;i&gt;at every stage&lt;/i&gt; of the measurement lifecycle. Representations should at least improve our understanding; ideally, they also make follow-up actions clear. Statements without context are poor representations. For example, â30% greater chanceâ sounds like a lot but has no value without a reference pointâ30% of 0.5% is likely less a concern than 30% of 20% chance.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;One example of representation is Cloudflareâs â&lt;a href=\&quot;https://www.cloudflare.com/sv-se/network/\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;closeness&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;â statement: Cloudflare is â&lt;i&gt;approximately 50 ms from 95% of the Internet-connected population globally&lt;/i&gt;.â The statement encapsulates a âsurveyâ of our logs: From among all connections from each IP address that connects to Cloudflare, half of the minimum-RTT is a âworst approximationâ of the latency from the IP address to Cloudflare; in 95% of cases, the minRTT/2 is at or below 50ms.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;Visualizations, meanwhile, can be so powerful as to lead to misleading conclusions â a notion that features prominently later this week in a blog post about routing resilience evaluations. One example on that subject appears below, with two bar charts that order individual US states by the number of interconnection facilities in each state, from most to least. On the left, states are ordered according to raw count facilities; the top-ranked state has more than 140 interconnection facilities. On the right, the raw counts are normalized (in this case divided by) the population of each state.&lt;/p&gt;\n          &lt;figure class=\&quot;kg-card kg-image-card\&quot;&gt;\n          &lt;Image src=\&quot;https://cf-assets.www.cloudflare.com/zkvhlag99gkb/7c2LwnBXvucFPhWKwG0F7g/033d94a2a8e3be8844a6f958ced6d762/image9.png\&quot; alt=\&quot;\&quot; class=\&quot;kg-image\&quot; width=\&quot;2047\&quot; height=\&quot;678\&quot; loading=\&quot;lazy\&quot;/&gt;\n          &lt;/figure&gt;&lt;p&gt;These representations demonstrate that our models are shaped, and can be misinformed, by how we evaluate data. In this case we have purposefully omitted the state names on the x-axis because they are a distraction. Instead, each bar is coloured to indicate whether it is above (green) or below (yellow) the median of facilities per person in the right-hand graph. What becomes immediately obvious is that the two states with the highest number of facilities fall below the median, i.e., they are in the bottom half of states when ordered by facilities per person.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;Sometimes a visualization can be so powerful as to leave no doubt. The image below is a personal favourite, because it gives strong evidence that the data and models were correct. In this visualization, each column represents a single type of connection anomaly that we observed. Inside each column, the anomalyâs occurrence is divided proportionally into the country where the connection was initiated. As an example, look at the left-most column for SYNââ anomalies (a type of timeout). It shows that connections from China, India, Iran, and the United States dominated this specific anomaly type. Organizing the visualization this way put the data &lt;i&gt;first&lt;/i&gt;, which helped mitigate any bias we might have had about explanations, underlying mechanisms, or locations.&lt;/p&gt;\n          &lt;figure class=\&quot;kg-card kg-image-card\&quot;&gt;\n          &lt;Image src=\&quot;https://cf-assets.www.cloudflare.com/zkvhlag99gkb/1sUdADDxOjZzn5Bq6qCgs2/0cfd453013b83ff50924993bb38c6e9b/image1.png\&quot; alt=\&quot;\&quot; class=\&quot;kg-image\&quot; width=\&quot;1575\&quot; height=\&quot;809\&quot; loading=\&quot;lazy\&quot;/&gt;\n          &lt;/figure&gt;&lt;p&gt;By organizing the anomalies this way, the visualization immediately answered one question: âAre the failures expected behaviour?âÂ  If they were expected, or normal across the Internet, then the anomalies would appear in roughly similar proportions rather than so different. The visualization was a strong validation (but &lt;a href=\&quot;https://blog.cloudflare.com/connection-tampering/#signature-validation-letting-the-data-speak\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;not the only one&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;) of our approach and intuitionâand opened up further avenues of investigation as a result.&lt;/p&gt;\n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h2 id=\&quot;whats-next\&quot;&gt;Whatâs next?&lt;/h2&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#whats-next\&quot; aria-hidden=\&quot;true\&quot; class=\&quot;relative sm:absolute sm:-left-5\&quot;&gt;\n        &lt;svg width=\&quot;16\&quot; height=\&quot;16\&quot; viewBox=\&quot;0 0 24 24\&quot;&gt;&lt;path fill=\&quot;currentcolor\&quot; d=\&quot;m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z\&quot;&gt;&lt;/path&gt;&lt;/svg&gt;\n      &lt;/a&gt;\n    &lt;/div&gt;\n    &lt;p&gt;Cloudflare continues to think deeply about new and novel ways to use available (passive) data, and welcomes ideas. Measurement helps us understand the Internet we all depend on, value, and love, and is a community-wide endeavour.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;We encourage new entrants into the measurement space, and hope this blog serves as both an introduction to its challenges, and a map with which to evaluate measurement work published at Cloudflare or anywhere else.&lt;/p&gt;&quot;],&quot;published_at&quot;:[0,&quot;2025-10-27T13:00+00:00&quot;],&quot;updated_at&quot;:[0,&quot;2025-10-27T20:44:54.115Z&quot;],&quot;feature_image&quot;:[0,&quot;https://cf-assets.www.cloudflare.com/zkvhlag99gkb/3N92MMX3fJfZY5VQuyKdgg/28b943e922f74e0ded62904c1388b3fa/image7.png&quot;],&quot;tags&quot;:[1,[[0,{&quot;id&quot;:[0,&quot;1x7tpPmKIUCt19EDgM1Tsl&quot;],&quot;name&quot;:[0,&quot;Research&quot;],&quot;slug&quot;:[0,&quot;research&quot;]}]]],&quot;relatedTags&quot;:[0],&quot;authors&quot;:[1,[[0,{&quot;name&quot;:[0,&quot;Marwan Fayed&quot;],&quot;slug&quot;:[0,&quot;marwan&quot;],&quot;bio&quot;:[0,null],&quot;profile_image&quot;:[0,&quot;https://cf-assets.www.cloudflare.com/zkvhlag99gkb/IXphrXdPbBBtMHdRMzIrB/340102a31143f4743a74c87ec0e0c11e/marwan.jpg&quot;],&quot;location&quot;:[0,null],&quot;website&quot;:[0,null],&quot;twitter&quot;:[0,&quot;@marwanfayed&quot;],&quot;facebook&quot;:[0,null],&quot;publiclyIndex&quot;:[0,true]}]]],&quot;meta_description&quot;:[0,&quot;The Internet is one big open system composed of many closed boxes â which makes measuring the Internet difficult. justify-center pt2 pt4-l"><article class="post-full mw-100 ph3 ph0-l fs-20px"><h1 class="f6 f7-l fw4 gray1 pt1 pt3-l mb1">Keeping the Internet fast and secure: introducing Merkle Tree Certificates</h1><p class="f3 fw5 gray5 db di-l mt2">2025-10-28</p><ul class="author-lists flex pl0 mt4"><li class="list flex items-center pr2 mb1-ns"><a href="/author/luke/" class="static-avatar pr1"><img class="author-profile-image br-100 mr2" src="https://blog.cloudflare.com/cdn-cgi/image/format=auto,dpr=3,width=64,height=64,gravity=face,fit=crop,zoom=0.5/https://cf-assets.www.cloudflare.com/zkvhlag99gkb/2FfvsynZPyK2w2lJSIXdqr/f8758aa6638121491d233932a076770e/luke.jpg" alt="Luke Valenta" width="62" height="62"/></a><div class="author-name-tooltip"><a href="/author/luke/" class="fw4 f3 no-underline black mr3">Luke Valenta</a></div></li><li class="list flex items-center pr2 mb1-ns"><a href="/author/christopher-patton/" class="static-avatar pr1"><img class="author-profile-image br-100 mr2" src="https://blog.cloudflare.com/cdn-cgi/image/format=auto,dpr=3,width=64,height=64,gravity=face,fit=crop,zoom=0.5/https://cf-assets.www.cloudflare.com/zkvhlag99gkb/4sRmXhNpfDUovdIUpP9Keq/8dfd4dfc04f8f6d45b0a69121edfa0e2/christopher-patton.jpg" alt="Christopher Patton" width="62" height="62"/></a><div class="author-name-tooltip"><a href="/author/christopher-patton/" class="fw4 f3 no-underline black mr3">Christopher Patton</a></div></li><li class="list flex items-center pr2 mb1-ns"><a href="/author/vania/" class="static-avatar pr1"><img class="author-profile-image br-100 mr2" src="https://blog.cloudflare.com/cdn-cgi/image/format=auto,dpr=3,width=64,height=64,gravity=face,fit=crop,zoom=0.5/https://cf-assets.www.cloudflare.com/zkvhlag99gkb/6q2dj1Og6MibXRPbTwEqoP/beec340219225c4d2198f976e0bdefee/vania.jpg" alt="VÃ¢nia GonÃ§alves" width="62" height="62"/></a><div class="author-name-tooltip"><a href="/author/vania/" class="fw4 f3 no-underline black mr3">VÃ¢nia GonÃ§alves</a></div></li><li class="list flex items-center pr2 mb1-ns"><a href="/author/bas/" class="static-avatar pr1"><img class="author-profile-image br-100 mr2" src="https://blog.cloudflare.com/cdn-cgi/image/format=auto,dpr=3,width=64,height=64,gravity=face,fit=crop,zoom=0.5/https://cf-assets.www.cloudflare.com/zkvhlag99gkb/4KeBG8XO1ADZHuEV9v5Hwz/6c90a96cfb1373b208bffc35f3fd71f4/bas.png" alt="Bas Westerbaan" width="62" height="62"/></a><div class="author-name-tooltip"><a href="/author/bas/" class="fw4 f3 no-underline black mr3">Bas Westerbaan</a></div></li></ul><section class="post-full-content"><div class="mb2 gray5">12 min read</div><img class="mr2" src="https://cf-assets.www.cloudflare.com/zkvhlag99gkb/1sJBsQ55nUSWxEq5SO0PS1/3265c773d1b11dafa78ea7e17354d408/image2.png" alt=""/><div class="post-content lh-copy gray1"><p>The world is in a race to build its first quantum computer capable of solving practical problems not feasible on even the largest conventional supercomputers. While the quantum computing paradigm promises many benefits, it also threatens the security of the Internet by breaking much of the cryptography we have come to rely on.</p><p>To mitigate this threat, Cloudflare is helping to migrate the Internet to Post-Quantum (PQ) cryptography. Today, <a href="https://radar.cloudflare.com/adoption-and-usage#post-quantum-encryption"><u>about 50%</u></a> of traffic to Cloudflare&#39;s edge network is protected against the most urgent threat: an attacker who can intercept and store encrypted traffic today and then decrypt it in the future with the help of a quantum computer. This is referred to as the <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harvest_now,_decrypt_later"><u>harvest now, decrypt later</u></a><i> </i>threat.</p><p>However, this is just one of the threats we need to address. A quantum computer can also be used to crack a server&#39;s <a href="https://www.cloudflare.com/learning/ssl/transport-layer-security-tls/"><u>TLS</u></a> certificate, allowing an attacker to impersonate the server to unsuspecting clients. The good news is that we already have PQ algorithms we can use for quantum-safe authentication. The bad news is that adoption of these algorithms in TLS will require significant changes to one of the most complex and security-critical systems on the Internet: the Web Public-Key Infrastructure (WebPKI).</p><p>The central problem is the sheer size of these new algorithms: signatures for ML-DSA-44, one of the most performant PQ algorithms standardized by NIST, are 2,420 bytes long, compared to just 64 bytes for ECDSA-P256, the most popular non-PQ signature in use today; and its public keys are 1,312 bytes long, compared to just 64 bytes for ECDSA. That&#39;s a roughly 20-fold increase in size. Worse yet, the average TLS handshake includes a number of public keys and signatures, adding up to 10s of kilobytes of overhead per handshake. This is enough to have a <a href="https://blog.cloudflare.com/another-look-at-pq-signatures/#how-many-added-bytes-are-too-many-for-tls"><u>noticeable impact</u></a> on the performance of TLS.</p><p>That makes drop-in PQ certificates a tough sell to enable today: they donât bring any security benefit before Q-day â the day a cryptographically relevant quantum computer arrives â but they do degrade performance. We could sit and wait until Q-day is a year away, but thatâs playing with fire. Migrations always take longer than expected, and by waiting we risk the security and privacy of the Internet, which is <a href="https://developers.cloudflare.com/ssl/edge-certificates/universal-ssl/"><u>dear to us</u></a>.</p><p>It&#39;s clear that we must find a way to make post-quantum certificates cheap enough to deploy today by default for everyone â not just those that can afford it. In this post, we&#39;ll introduce you to the plan weâve brought together with industry partners to the <a href="https://datatracker.ietf.org/group/plants/about/"><u>IETF</u></a> to redesign the WebPKI in order to allow a smooth transition to PQ authentication with no performance impact (and perhaps a performance improvement!). We&#39;ll provide an overview of one concrete proposal, called <a href="https://datatracker.ietf.org/doc/draft-davidben-tls-merkle-tree-certs/"><u>Merkle Tree Certificates (MTCs)</u></a>, whose goal is to whittle down the number of public keys and signatures in the TLS handshake to the bare minimum required.</p><p>But talk is cheap. We <a href="https://blog.cloudflare.com/experiment-with-pq/"><u>know</u></a> <a href="https://blog.cloudflare.com/announcing-encrypted-client-hello/"><u>from</u></a> <a href="https://blog.cloudflare.com/why-tls-1-3-isnt-in-browsers-yet/"><u>experience</u></a> that, as with any change to the Internet, it&#39;s crucial to test early and often. <b>Today we&#39;re announcing our intent to deploy MTCs on an experimental basis in collaboration with Chrome Security.</b> In this post, we&#39;ll describe the scope of this experiment, what we hope to learn from it, and how we&#39;ll make sure it&#39;s done safely.</p>
    <div class="flex anchor relative">
      <h2 id="the-webpki-today-an-old-system-with-many-patches">The WebPKI today â an old system with many patches</h2>
      <a href="#the-webpki-today-an-old-system-with-many-patches" aria-hidden="true" class="relative sm:absolute sm:-left-5">
        <svg width="16" height="16" viewBox="0 0 24 24"><path fill="currentcolor" d="m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z"></path></svg>
      </a>
    </div>
    <p>Why does the TLS handshake have so many public keys and signatures?</p><p>Let&#39;s start with Cryptography 101. When your browser connects to a website, it asks the server to <b>authenticate</b> itself to make sure it&#39;s talking to the real server and not an impersonator. This is usually achieved with a cryptographic primitive known as a digital signature scheme (e.g., ECDSA or ML-DSA). In TLS, the server signs the messages exchanged between the client and server using its <b>secret key</b>, and the client verifies the signature using the server&#39;s <b>public key</b>. In this way, the server confirms to the client that they&#39;ve had the same conversation, since only the server could have produced a valid signature.</p><p>If the client already knows the server&#39;s public key, then only <b>1 signature</b> is required to authenticate the server. In practice, however, this is not really an option. The web today is made up of around a billion TLS servers, so it would be unrealistic to provision every client with the public key of every server. What&#39;s more, the set of public keys will change over time as new servers come online and existing ones rotate their keys, so we would need some way of pushing these changes to clients.</p><p>This scaling problem is at the heart of the design of all PKIs.</p>
    <div class="flex anchor relative">
      <h3 id="trust-is-transitive">Trust is transitive</h3>
      <a href="#trust-is-transitive" aria-hidden="true" class="relative sm:absolute sm:-left-5">
        <svg width="16" height="16" viewBox="0 0 24 24"><path fill="currentcolor" d="m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z"></path></svg>
      </a>
    </div>
    <p>Instead of expecting the client to know the server&#39;s public key in advance, the server might just send its public key during the TLS handshake. But how does the client know that the public key actually belongs to the server? This is the job of a <b>certificate</b>.</p><p>A certificate binds a public key to the identity of the server â usually its DNS name, e.g., <code>cloudflareresearch.com</code>. The certificate is signed by a Certification Authority (CA) whose public key is known to the client. In addition to verifying the server&#39;s handshake signature, the client verifies the signature of this certificate. This establishes a chain of trust: by accepting the certificate, the client is trusting that the CA verified that the public key actually belongs to the server with that identity.</p><p>Clients are typically configured to trust many CAs and must be provisioned with a public key for each. Things are much easier however, since there are only 100s of CAs instead of billions. In addition, new certificates can be created without having to update clients.</p><p>These efficiencies come at a relatively low cost: for those counting at home, that&#39;s <b>+1</b> signature and <b>+1</b> public key, for a total of <b>2 signatures and 1 public key</b> per TLS handshake.</p><p>That&#39;s not the end of the story, however. As the WebPKI has evolved, so have these chains of trust grown a bit longer. These days it&#39;s common for a chain to consist of two or more certificates rather than just one. This is because CAs sometimes need to rotate<b> </b>their keys, just as servers do. But before they can start using the new key, they must distribute the corresponding public key to clients. This takes time, since it requires billions of clients to update their trust stores. To bridge the gap, the CA will sometimes use the old key to issue a certificate for the new one and append this certificate to the end of the chain.</p><p>That&#39;s<b> +1</b> signature and<b> +1</b> public key, which brings us to<b> 3 signatures and 2 public keys</b>. And we still have a little ways to go.</p>
    <div class="flex anchor relative">
      <h3 id="trust-but-verify">Trust but verify</h3>
      <a href="#trust-but-verify" aria-hidden="true" class="relative sm:absolute sm:-left-5">
        <svg width="16" height="16" viewBox="0 0 24 24"><path fill="currentcolor" d="m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z"></path></svg>
      </a>
    </div>
    <p>The main job of a CA is to verify that a server has control over the domain for which itâs requesting a certificate. This process has evolved over the years from a high-touch, CA-specific process to a standardized, <a href="https://datatracker.ietf.org/doc/html/rfc8555/"><u>mostly automated process</u></a> used for issuing most certificates on the web. (Not all CAs fully support automation, however.) This evolution is marked by a number of security incidents in which a certificate was <b>mis-issued </b>to a party other than the server, allowing that party to impersonate the server to any client that trusts the CA.</p><p>Automation helps, but <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/DigiNotar#Issuance_of_fraudulent_certificates"><u>attacks</u></a> are still possible, and mistakes are almost inevitable. <a href="https://blog.cloudflare.com/unauthorized-issuance-of-certificates-for-1-1-1-1/"><u>Earlier this year</u></a>, several certificates for Cloudflare&#39;s encrypted 1.1.1.1 resolver were issued without our involvement or authorization. This apparently occurred by accident, but it nonetheless put users of 1.1.1.1 at risk. (The mis-issued certificates have since been revoked.)</p><p>Ensuring mis-issuance is detectable is the job of the Certificate Transparency (CT) ecosystem. The basic idea is that each certificate issued by a CA gets added to a public <b>log</b>. Servers can audit these logs for certificates issued in their name. If ever a certificate is issued that they didn&#39;t request itself, the server operator can prove the issuance happened, and the PKI ecosystem can take action to prevent the certificate from being trusted by clients.</p><p>Major browsers, including Firefox and Chrome and its derivatives, require certificates to be logged before they can be trusted. For example, Chrome, Safari, and Firefox will only accept the server&#39;s certificate if it appears in at least two logs the browser is configured to trust. This policy is easy to state, but tricky to implement in practice:</p><ol><li><p>Operating a CT log has historically been fairly expensive. Logs ingest billions of certificates over their lifetimes: when an incident happens, or even just under high load, it can take some time for a log to make a new entry available for auditors.</p></li><li><p>Clients can&#39;t really audit logs themselves, since this would expose their browsing history (i.e., the servers they wanted to connect to) to the log operators.</p></li></ol><p>The solution to both problems is to include a signature from the CT log along with the certificate. The signature is produced immediately in response to a request to log a certificate, and attests to the log&#39;s intent to include the certificate in the log within 24 hours.</p><p>Per browser policy, certificate transparency adds <b>+2</b> signatures to the TLS handshake, one for each log. This brings us to a total of <b>5 signatures and 2 public keys</b> in a typical handshake on the public web.</p>
    <div class="flex anchor relative">
      <h3 id="the-future-webpki">The future WebPKI</h3>
      <a href="#the-future-webpki" aria-hidden="true" class="relative sm:absolute sm:-left-5">
        <svg width="16" height="16" viewBox="0 0 24 24"><path fill="currentcolor" d="m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z"></path></svg>
      </a>
    </div>
    <p>The WebPKI is a living, breathing, and highly distributed system. We&#39;ve had to patch it a number of times over the years to keep it going, but on balance it has served our needs quite well â until now.</p><p>Previously, whenever we needed to update something in the WebPKI, we would tack on another signature. This strategy has worked because conventional cryptography is so cheap. But <b>5 signatures and 2 public keys </b>on average for each TLS handshake is simply too much to cope with for the larger PQ signatures that are coming.</p><p>The good news is that by moving what we already have around in clever ways, we can drastically reduce the number of signatures we need.</p>
    <div class="flex anchor relative">
      <h3 id="crash-course-on-merkle-tree-certificates">Crash course on Merkle Tree Certificates</h3>
      <a href="#crash-course-on-merkle-tree-certificates" aria-hidden="true" class="relative sm:absolute sm:-left-5">
        <svg width="16" height="16" viewBox="0 0 24 24"><path fill="currentcolor" d="m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z"></path></svg>
      </a>
    </div>
    <p><a href="https://datatracker.ietf.org/doc/draft-davidben-tls-merkle-tree-certs/"><u>Merkle Tree Certificates (MTCs)</u></a> is a proposal for the next generation of the WebPKI that we are implementing and plan to deploy on an experimental basis. Its key features are as follows:</p><ol><li><p>All the information a client needs to validate a Merkle Tree Certificate can be disseminated out-of-band. If the client is sufficiently up-to-date, then the TLS handshake needs just <b>1 signature, 1 public key, and 1 Merkle tree inclusion proof</b>. This is quite small, even if we use post-quantum algorithms.</p></li><li><p>The MTC specification makes certificate transparency a first class feature of the PKI by having each CA run its own log of exactly the certificates they issue.</p></li></ol><p>Let&#39;s poke our head under the hood a little. Below we have an MTC generated by one of our internal tests. This would be transmitted from the server to the client in the TLS handshake:</p>
            <pre class="language-Rust"><code class="language-Rust">-----BEGIN CERTIFICATE-----
MIICSzCCAUGgAwIBAgICAhMwDAYKKwYBBAGC2ksvADAcMRowGAYKKwYBBAGC2ksv
AQwKNDQzNjMuNDguMzAeFw0yNTEwMjExNTMzMjZaFw0yNTEwMjgxNTMzMjZaMCEx
HzAdBgNVBAMTFmNsb3VkZmxhcmVyZXNlYXJjaC5jb20wWTATBgcqhkjOPQIBBggq

mibA021dDCi8h1hYFQAA
-----END CERTIFICATE-----</pre></code>
            <p>Looks like your average PEM encoded certificate. Let&#39;s decode it and look at the parameters:</p>
            <pre class="language-Text"><code class="language-Text">$ openssl x509 -in merkle-tree-cert.pem -noout -text

    Data:
        Version: 3 (0x2)
        Serial Number: 531 (0x213)

                pub:
                    04:70:ed:e1:96:87:b4:22:ef:fb:dc:a9:cd:9c:5c:

    Signature Value:
        00:00:00:00:00:00:02:00:00:00:00:00:00:00:02:58:00:e0:
        44:be:03:a5:bd:6a:b7:f2:9e:39:77:4c:16:4c:f8:06:e5:e1:
        55:c0:93:21:c6:79:83:3c:dd:5b:e6:57:89:c0:75:b3:4c:ec:
        75:8a:0b:53:a0:ca:1c:07:0c:1a:92:dd:c7:7c:a2:23:5d:83:
        0e:e4:23:43:38:af:43:20:a8:66:44:34:95:87:ea:2b:f0:0f:
        16:52:bb:ea:67:67:1e:89:36:4f:90:d4:05:55:89:46:f1:b7:
        b6:68:84:d3:57:31:ae:2b:c3:79:31:86:85:9d:24:ed:cf:25:
        a4:5c:fd:8f:f6:76:14:55:dd:67:2e:df:d6:8c:25:0d:52:48:
        c8:e3:fe:f9:7c:e6:a5:30:52:a5:b5:c7:3a:89:a5:c1:f6:4b:
        5b:95:ef:70:b8:91:fc:61:0f:6d:16:de:39:e9:a0:59:49:2b:
        34:71:7c:2a:16:da:c7:af:de:f7:01:94:10:c4:62:d1:f5:00:
        87:bd:e8:a2:f4:df:3b:35:79:27:0e:fc:cc:43:e7:60:5a:df:
        df:06:e8:d3:7e:eb:b3:bf:7b:25:43:0f:34:9a:26:c0:d3:6d:
        5d:0c:28:bc:87:58:58:15:00:00</pre></code>
            <p>While some of the parameters probably look familiar, others will look unusual. On the familiar side, the subject and public key are exactly what we might expect: the DNS name is <code>cloudflareresearch.com</code> and the public key is for a familiar signature algorithm, ECDSA-P256. This algorithm is not PQ, of course â in the future we would put ML-DSA-44 there instead.</p><p>On the unusual side, OpenSSL appears to not recognize the signature algorithm of the issuer and just prints the raw OID and bytes of the signature. There&#39;s a good reason for this: the MTC does not have a signature in it at all! So what exactly are we looking at?</p><p>The trick to leave out signatures is that a Merkle Tree Certification Authority (MTCA) produces its <i>signatureless</i> certificates <i>in batches</i> rather than individually. In place of a signature, the certificate has an <b>inclusion proof</b> of the certificate in a batch of certificates signed by the MTCA.</p><p>To understand how inclusion proofs work, let&#39;s think about a slightly simplified version of the MTC specification. To issue a batch, the MTCA arranges the unsigned certificates into a data structure called a <b>Merkle tree</b> that looks like this:</p>
          <figure class="kg-card kg-image-card">
          <Image src="https://cf-assets.www.cloudflare.com/zkvhlag99gkb/4LGhISsS07kbpSgDkqx8p2/68e3b36deeca7f97139654d2c769df68/image3.png" alt="" class="kg-image" width="1494" height="1026" loading="lazy"/>
          </figure><p>Each leaf of the tree corresponds to a certificate, and each inner node is equal to the hash of its children. To sign the batch, the MTCA uses its secret key to sign the head of the tree. The structure of the tree guarantees that each certificate in the batch was signed by the MTCA: if we tried to tweak the bits of any one of the certificates, the treehead would end up having a different value, which would cause the signature to fail.</p><p>An inclusion proof for a certificate consists of the hash of each sibling node along the path from the certificate to the treehead:</p>
          <figure class="kg-card kg-image-card">
          <Image src="https://cf-assets.www.cloudflare.com/zkvhlag99gkb/4UZZHkRwsBLWXRYeop4rXv/8598cde48c27c112bc4992889f3d5799/image1.gif" alt="" class="kg-image" width="1494" height="1026" loading="lazy"/>
          </figure><p>Given a validated treehead, this sequence of hashes is sufficient to prove inclusion of the certificate in the tree. This means that, in order to validate an MTC, the client also needs to obtain the signed treehead from the MTCA.</p><p>This is the key to MTC&#39;s efficiency:</p><ol><li><p>Signed treeheads can be disseminated to clients out-of-band and validated offline. Each validated treehead can then be used to validate any certificate in the corresponding batch, eliminating the need to obtain a signature for each server certificate.</p></li><li><p>During the TLS handshake, the client tells the server which treeheads it has. If the server has a signatureless certificate covered by one of those treeheads, then it can use that certificate to authenticate itself. That&#39;s <b>1 signature,1 public key and 1 inclusion proof</b> per handshake, both for the server being authenticated.</p></li></ol><p>Now, that&#39;s the simplified version. MTC proper has some more bells and whistles. To start, it doesnât create a separate Merkle tree for each batch, but it grows a single large tree, which is used for better transparency. As this tree grows, periodically (sub)tree heads are selected to be shipped to browsers, which we call <b>landmarks</b>. In the common case browsers will be able to fetch the most recent landmarks, and servers can wait for batch issuance, but we need a fallback: MTC also supports certificates that can be issued immediately and donât require landmarks to be validated, but these are not as small. A server would provision both types of Merkle tree certificates, so that the common case is fast, and the exceptional case is slow, but at least itâll work.</p>
    <div class="flex anchor relative">
      <h2 id="experimental-deployment">Experimental deployment</h2>
      <a href="#experimental-deployment" aria-hidden="true" class="relative sm:absolute sm:-left-5">
        <svg width="16" height="16" viewBox="0 0 24 24"><path fill="currentcolor" d="m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z"></path></svg>
      </a>
    </div>
    <p>Ever since early designs for MTCs emerged, weâve been eager to experiment with the idea. In line with the IETF principle of â<a href="https://www.ietf.org/runningcode/"><u>running code</u></a>â, it often takes implementing a protocol to work out kinks in the design. At the same time, we cannot risk the security of users. In this section, we describe our approach to experimenting with aspects of the Merkle Tree Certificates design <i>without</i> changing any trust relationships.</p><p>Letâs start with what we hope to learn. We have lots of questions whose answers can help to either validate the approach, or uncover pitfalls that require reshaping the protocol â in fact, an implementation of an early MTC draft by <a href="https://www.cs.ru.nl/masters-theses/2025/M_Pohl___Implementation_and_Analysis_of_Merkle_Tree_Certificates_for_Post-Quantum_Secure_Authentication_in_TLS.pdf"><u>Maximilian Pohl</u></a> and <a href="https://www.ietf.org/archive/id/draft-davidben-tls-merkle-tree-certs-07.html#name-acknowledgements"><u>Mia Celeste</u></a> did exactly this. Weâd like to know:</p><p><b>What breaks?</b> Protocol ossification (the tendency of implementation bugs to make it harder to change a protocol) is an ever-present issue with deploying protocol changes. For TLS in particular, despite having built-in flexibility, time after time weâve found that if that flexibility is not regularly used, there will be buggy implementations and middleboxes that break when they see things they donât recognize. TLS 1.3 deployment <a href="https://blog.cloudflare.com/why-tls-1-3-isnt-in-browsers-yet/"><u>took years longer</u></a> than we hoped for this very reason. And more recently, the rollout of PQ key exchange in TLS caused the Client Hello to be split over multiple TCP packets, something that many middleboxes <a href="https://tldr.fail/"><u>weren&#39;t ready for</u></a>.</p><p><b>What is the performance impact?</b> In fact, we expect MTCs to <i>reduce </i>the size of the handshake, even compared to today&#39;s non-PQ certificates. They will also reduce CPU cost: ML-DSA signature verification is about as fast as ECDSA, and there will be far fewer signatures to verify. We therefore expect to see a <i>reduction in latency</i>. We would like to see if there is a measurable performance improvement.</p><p><b>What fraction of clients will stay up to date? </b>Getting the performance benefit of MTCs requires the clients and servers to be roughly in sync with one another. We expect MTCs to have fairly short lifetimes, a week or so. This means that if the client&#39;s latest landmark is older than a week, the server would have to fallback to a larger certificate. Knowing how often this fallback happens will help us tune the parameters of the protocol to make fallbacks less likely.</p><p>In order to answer these questions, we are implementing MTC support in our TLS stack and in our certificate issuance infrastructure. For their part, Chrome is implementing MTC support in their own TLS stack and will stand up infrastructure to disseminate landmarks to their users.</p><p>As we&#39;ve done in past experiments, we plan to enable MTCs for a subset of our free customers with enough traffic that we will be able to get useful measurements. Chrome will control the experimental rollout: they can ramp up slowly, measuring as they go and rolling back if and when bugs are found.</p><p>Which leaves us with one last question: who will run the Merkle Tree CA?</p>
    <div class="flex anchor relative">
      <h3 id="bootstrapping-trust-from-the-existing-webpki">Bootstrapping trust from the existing WebPKI</h3>
      <a href="#bootstrapping-trust-from-the-existing-webpki" aria-hidden="true" class="relative sm:absolute sm:-left-5">
        <svg width="16" height="16" viewBox="0 0 24 24"><path fill="currentcolor" d="m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z"></path></svg>
      </a>
    </div>
    <p>Standing up a proper CA is no small task: it takes years to be trusted by major browsers. Thatâs why Cloudflare isnât going to become a ârealâ CA for this experiment, and Chrome isnât going to trust us directly.</p><p>Instead, to make progress on a reasonable timeframe, without sacrificing due diligence, we plan to &quot;mock&quot; the role of the MTCA. We will run an MTCA (on <a href="https://github.com/cloudflare/azul/"><u>Workers</u></a> based on our <a href="https://blog.cloudflare.com/azul-certificate-transparency-log/"><u>StaticCT logs</u></a>), but for each MTC we issue, we also publish an existing certificate from a trusted CA that agrees with it. We call this the <b>bootstrap certificate</b>. When Chromeâs infrastructure pulls updates from our MTCA log, they will also pull these bootstrap certificates, and check whether they agree. Only if they do, theyâll proceed to push the corresponding landmarks to Chrome clients. In other words, Cloudflare is effectively just âre-encodingâ an existing certificate (with domain validation performed by a trusted CA) as an MTC, and Chrome is using certificate transparency to keep us honest.</p>
    <div class="flex anchor relative">
      <h2 id="conclusion">Conclusion</h2>
      <a href="#conclusion" aria-hidden="true" class="relative sm:absolute sm:-left-5">
        <svg width="16" height="16" viewBox="0 0 24 24"><path fill="currentcolor" d="m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z"></path></svg>
      </a>
    </div>
    <p>With almost 50% of our traffic already protected by post-quantum encryption, weâre halfway to a fully post-quantum secure Internet. The second part of our journey, post-quantum certificates, is the hardest yet though. A simple drop-in upgrade has a noticeable performance impact and no security benefit before Q-day. This means itâs a hard sell to enable today by default. But here we are playing with fire: migrations always take longer than expected. If we want to keep an ubiquitously private and secure Internet, we need a post-quantum solution thatâs performant enough to be enabled by default <b>today</b>.</p><p>Merkle Tree Certificates (MTCs) solves this problem by reducing the number of signatures and public keys to the bare minimum while maintaining the WebPKI&#39;s essential properties. We plan to roll out MTCs to a fraction of free accounts by early next year. This does not affect any visitors that are not part of the Chrome experiment. For those that are, thanks to the bootstrap certificates, there is no impact on security.</p><p>Weâre excited to keep the Internet fast <i>and</i> secure, and will report back soon on the results of this experiment: watch this space! MTC is evolving as we speak, if you want to get involved, please join the IETF <a href="https://mailman3.ietf.org/mailman3/lists/plants@ietf.org/"><u>PLANTS mailing list</u></a>.</p></div></section><section class="post-full-content flex flex-row flex-wrap mw7 center mb4"><div class="post-content lh-copy w-100 gray1 bt b--gray8 pt4">Cloudflare's connectivity cloud protects <a target='_blank' href='https://www.cloudflare.com/network-services/' rel='noreferrer'>entire corporate networks</a>, helps customers build <a target='_blank' href='https://workers.cloudflare.com/' rel='noreferrer'>Internet-scale applications efficiently</a>, accelerates any <a target='_blank' href='https://www.cloudflare.com/performance/accelerate-internet-applications/' rel='noreferrer'>website or Internet application</a>, <a target='_blank' href='https://www.cloudflare.com/ddos/' rel='noreferrer'>wards off DDoS attacks</a>, keeps <a target='_blank' href='https://www.cloudflare.com/application-security/' rel='noreferrer'>hackers at bay</a>, and can help you on <a target='_blank' href='https://www.cloudflare.com/products/zero-trust/' rel='noreferrer'>your journey to Zero Trust</a>.<br/><br/>Visit <a target='_blank' href='https://one.one.one.one/' rel='noreferrer'>1.1.1.1</a> from any device to get started with our free app that makes your Internet faster and safer.<br/><br/>To learn more about our mission to help build a better Internet, <a target='_blank' href='https://www.cloudflare.com/learning/what-is-cloudflare/' rel='noreferrer'>start here</a>.
